#2908. Can the Sixers keep it rolling against challenging opponents?

For as bad as last Friday's game was in Cleveland, the Sixers probably deserve a mulligan. At 9-2 after Sunday's win in Chicago, Philly has not only looked the part of a championship contender, but they've done it against lots of fellow contenders, Sunday notwithstanding. The schedule is about to lighten up for the Sixers, but they still have two more games this week against playoff teams from last season. They'll host Boston on Tuesday and Toronto on Thursday.

If the Sixers can get to 11-2, they'll be set up to face a slew of sub-.500 teams for the rest of November. They've already got the attention of the entire NBA world given that they've lived up to the preseason hype thus far. Think about what the narrative could be in 3-4 week's time surrounding this team. The wins should continue to be piled up, and they would have already beaten all the top challengers in the Eastern Conference once already and it wouldn't even be Christmas yet.

Oddly enough, the Sixers have not had home-court advantage in a playoff series since the 2023 first round when they swept Brooklyn. That means it hasn't happened in three previous seasons under Nick Nurse. As they continue to stockpile victories early in the season, the reality of a deep playoff run becomes increasingly plausible. In two postseasons under Nurse, the Sixers have had grueling first-round matchups, losing a tight series to New York in 2024 and having to rally from 3-1 down to eliminate Boston in 2026. The drain of having to dig out of the 3-1 hole against Boston clearly showed itself in the second round last spring when the Sixers got whitewashed by the Knicks in a quick four-game sweep.

In 2023, Philly was ahead 3-2 after five games against Boston and on the precipice of making the Eastern Conference Finals for the first time since 2001. We all know how the series ended, but the point remains that getting through an easy first-round series would do wonders for getting Joel Embiid — and LeBron James at his advanced age, for that matter — through an entire four rounds of the postseason.

As the Sixers continue to stack victories early in the 2026-27 season, they'll continue to make the path to an NBA Championship easier for themselves. Not only have they been stacking victories but finishing off such a challenging first three weeks of the season with two more wins this week against Boston and Toronto would only be the icing on a cake that's already baking pretty good. In addition to making their postseason path easier, Philly could be as selective as possible in determining when to load manage Embiid and James.

The NBA regular season has often come with a label of irrelevance thanks in part to the load managing we see around the league with superstars and their playing time. While it feels like the Sixers have washed away a lot of the negative labels surrounding their ability to make a deep playoff run, there probably is a segment of the fanbase that is still very much in "Wake me up in April" mode and, to some extent, that's an understandable sentiment to feel.

But the Sixers are doing all they can possibly do now to make fans believe that this could be the year they play into June. They're beating every contender in front of them. Remember, despite Friday's drubbing in Cleveland, they came back from a 29-point deficit to beat the Cavaliers earlier in the season. Simply put, it's a great time to be a Sixers fan, even if it's only November.

#2907. Trump says Jensen Huang told him Monday he didn't know paramour 'was a spy'

President Donald Trump said he spoke to Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Monday, and that Huang denied knowing that the woman he admitted having an extramarital affair with "was a spy" for China.

Trump told reporters at the White House that he was "amazed" by the allegations against Huang, which led Nvidia's board to initiate an independent investigation.

Trump suggested he had only recently learned of allegations that Megaspeed International executive Alice Huang was a Chinese spy.

"I didn't hear about it then, but a lot of people have heard about it, and Jensen totally denies that," Trump said.

"I asked him today because I did speak to him today just to see if he's feeling all right. 'How you doing?'" Trump said.

"And I asked him about that, and he ... said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," the president said. "I asked him that question: 'Did you have any idea that she was a spy?' He said, 'I had no idea that she was a spy.' "

The New York Times on Oct. 29 reported that Alice Huang had been eyed by U.S. spy agencies "for possibly steering advanced Nvidia A.I. chips to China," and that "American officials had concluded she was a Chinese intelligence asset, funneling information to Beijing."

The Times reported that about a year ago, Trump was briefed about the intelligence findings "and chose not to do anything with the information." The newspaper noted that Trump has been reluctant to disrupt chip sales by Nvdia, which are boosting the U.S. stock market.

On Friday, The Times reported that a memo that circulated last spring among top Trump administration policymakers "cited intelligence that a Chinese businesswoman who had a close relationship with Nvidia's chief executive was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security."

The Times said the memo reported that Alice Huang was recruited to collect intelligence on the Nvidia CEO "as part of a drive by Beijing's top spy agency to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China."

"I've been reading a little bit about it over a period of time, but not too long ago," Trump said Monday. "We'll look into it. I know the FBI is looking into it in terms of something else, and I think that he'll be in good shape."

Trump on Monday said of Jensen Huang, "He's suffering greatly."

"I believe he's suffering greatly. I read his statement, and I believe he's suffering greatly, more so because of his family, his wife, who he loves, and his family. But he is definitely suffering."

Asked if he would bring up the issue involving Huang with Chinese President Xi Jinping when the two leaders meet next week at the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen, China, Trump said, "I don't think so."

"I mean, maybe I'm sure President Xi will ask me about it," Trump said. "I just know Jensen very well. I just think he's a very honorable, honorable man. I mean ... he built a great company, American company, and I think he's a very honorable guy, and I feel very confident that he'll be in good shape."

#2905. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2904. Rockets need to reclaim their identity

The Houston Rockets are having an identity crisis.

Think Fight Club (which, as an aside, I only watched for the first time a few years ago, and if you haven't seen it, don't bother; it's been caricatured to the point that it feels like a parody of itself), in reverse. The Rockets were a rugged, fist-wielding Brad Pitt in 2025-26. Pesky social conventions like "getting good shots" and "running offensive sets" were boring and beneath them. The Rockets were winning fights instead of playing games.

In 2026-27, they're more like the neutered, emasculated Edward Norton. They've mostly been doing all the right things, and it's mostly been working, but they're just lacking some kind of cliched, overly white-male-privileged (blue wavy baby, we're back) existential purpose.

Let's park the analogy. It's good that the Rockets' offense has looked more sophisticated in 2026-27. They shouldn't abandon that. Instead, they need to synthesize last season's approach with this one.

In their last contest, the old 2025-26 offense reared its ugly (or maybe ruggedly handsome if we're Brad Pitt) head. The floor shrank. Instead of zipping high post passes to a cutting Amen Thompson, Alperen Sengun settled for a bunch of bad floaters that he's never been proficient with, in large part because the Thompson cuts weren't coming. The Rockets' offense fell apart:

Yet, if they'd just dominated the Suns on the glass, they would have won anyway.

Rest assured, this offense will fall apart again.

Ultimately, it's the same old chestnut: The Rockets don't have an every-night, carry-the-offense, even if he's missing, he's making something happen superstar. Sorry, Sengun fans, and Thompson isn't even close, and it's just not how the team is using Kevin Durant this year, which makes sense from a longer-term developmental perspective and a shorter-term, preserve him for the playoffs perspective.

Last year, the Rockets compensated for that lack of top-end talent by dominating physically. They were number one by a long shot in Offensive Rebounding %. This year, they're fourth (36.0%). They're fifth in overall Rebounding % (52.5th), which they also led the NBA in last season.

Sure, the Rockets are still a great rebounding team. If they have 2026-27 NBA championship aspirations, great isn't great enough. They need to be the best rebounding team in the NBA, or else they will lose in the playoffs to a team with better players.

It's not just rebounding, either. The Rockets' 109.4 Defensive Rating ranks 10th in the NBA. Last year, they had the league's sixth-best defense (112.1). This year's mark is actually better, but that feels like a small-sample quirk. Their placement relative to the league seems more relevant.

Of course, they also have the second-best Offensive Rating in the NBA (118.6). Make no mistake: This year's team looks better than last year's team. It would also be wise to avoid overreacting to one game, but against the Suns, it felt like some offensive regression was due. If it is, they need to regress to the mean (a confusing phrase when something is improving; it ought to be progress to the mean, why don't we say that?) in rebounding and defense.

Some of this is due to Steven Adams' absence, but let's not make excuses. If Adams can't regularly play, the Rockets need a new backup five, and he needs to be the best rebounder they can find. The Rockets need to rediscover their identity:

After all, the things you do own, end up owning you.

#2902. Jensen Huang affair puts Nvidia's future at risk, with no clear successor to the renowned CEO

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's admission of an affair with a purported Chinese spy has raised questions about his future at the company, and what happens if the 63-year-old co-founder, who built the chipmaker into one of the most valuable businesses on the planet, is forced out.

Shares of Nvidia sank more than 4% on Monday, even as the broader market rallied sharply, after Huang acknowledged the affair in a message to staff on Sunday, and said that the board was launching an investigation into whether he was targeted by a foreign intelligence agency. The board said it has "confidence in Jensen," and that it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised."

The New York Times first reported that the Chinese businesswoman was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, according to people briefed on the intelligence circulated in a memo among the Trump administration in the spring. China has called the allegation a smear.

While Nvidia says Huang will continue to lead the company during the probe, pressure is rapidly mounting on the chipmaker, whose technology has powered the first four years of the generative artificial intelligence boom. Leaders from both parties are calling for briefings, and Democrats poised to take control of both houses of Congress are calling for investigations and new legislation "to stop advanced AI chips from fueling China's military," Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts wrote in a post on X on Monday.

Huang has outsized influence over Nvidia, and is the sole face of the company with no clear second-in-command. He's also among the loudest champions of AI in the country, is critical to the financing of the broader infrastructure buildout and has a close relationship with President Donald Trump, giving Nvidia a front-row seat when it comes to discussions on AI policy, safety and regulation.

"The issue at Nvidia is that there is no clear successor to Jensen, and we had expected him to stay in this role for at least 10 more years," analysts at Melius Research said in a Monday research note.

Stacy Rasgon, an analyst at Bernstein, told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" on Monday that, "Jensen is Nvidia. Nvidia is Jensen." He added that Huang "runs the thing pretty much top down," and "sort of pulls all the strings."

Nvidia didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

Nvidia has long maintained a flat, non-hierarchical organizational structure. Huang has deep involvement in the company's day-to-day operations and frequently serves as its spokesperson in front of world leaders and at industry conferences. In addition to being co-founder and CEO, Huang is also Nvidia's president.

In September of 2025, Huang told CNBC's Becky Quick that he had as many as 60 direct reports. Internal records reviewed by CNBC reveal that, as of this week, he had 33 direct reports, including CFO Colette Kress, influential Chief Scientist Bill Dally, senior vice president of software engineering Dwight Diercks, and Ian Buck, best known as the inventor of Nvidia's CUDA software.

However, relative to some of his founder peers in tech like Meta's Mark Zuckerberg and Elon Musk at SpaceX, Huang has minimal actual control. He owns just over 3% of the outstanding shares and is one of 11 members on a board that has no official chair.

And in the risk factors of Nvidia's financial filings, Huang isn't specifically identified as a key person. Rather, the company says, "we are highly dependent on the services of our longstanding executive team."

"Failure to ensure effective succession planning, transfer of knowledge, and smooth transitions involving executives and key employees could hinder our strategic planning, execution, and long-term success," Nvidia says.

Still, Huang's influence is undeniable, so much so that the question of succession is one that's hardly broached among analysts and investors.

Timm Schulze-Melander, an analyst at Rothschild & Co. Redburn, wrote in a Monday note that Huang could be forced to step back from the CEO role if the board's investigation reveals "something materially more serious" than an undisclosed relationship.

But Schulze-Melander said Huang's potential resignation isn't a foregone conclusion of the investigation, pointing to the importance of his leadership at the company.

Nvidia's board "needs to demonstrate independence and protect shareholders without destabilizing a company whose success is unusually dependent on its founder," Schulze-Melander wrote. "Its initial statement suggests it is attempting to achieve both."

Several analysts said the investigation could lead to governance changes at Nvidia, including putting a transition contingency plan in place.

The Melius analysts wrote that Nvidia has a "strong bench" of executive talent across its networking, hyperscale and high-performance computing businesses, along with "technical expertise" stemming from Chief Technology Officer Michael Kagan as well as Dally, the chief scientist.

"While these folks can keep the engine running, Jensen is one of a kind, and his energy will be needed to maintain confidence in the AI trade," the analysts wrote.

— CNBC's Kif Leswing, Kristina Partsinevelos and Michael Bloom contributed to this report.

#2900. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2895. The Celtics are replacing Jaylen Brown by committee

Since teaming up in the fall of 2017, Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum forged on in Beantown as the Boston Celtics shuffled through name after name around them.

Kyrie Irving. Gordon Hayward. Kemba Walker. Robert Williams III. Marcus Smart. Al Horford. Jrue Holiday. Kristaps Porzingis.

All of them came and went while Boston pursued a title — and eventually claimed one in emphatic fashion — behind its two young, then budding, and, finally, established stars on the wing.

A year after winning a championship in 2024, the Tatum-Brown-Holiday-Horford-Porzingis core dissolved as the latter three departed. Despite Tatum missing much of 2025-26 while recovering from a torn Achilles, the Celtics pivoted toward a new group around their two pillars. Then, after a bountiful nine-year partnership, Tatum and Brown split.

In exchange for Paul George and two first-round picks (and two swaps), Boston dealt Brown to the Philadelphia 76ers over the summer. Brown's exit represented the Celtics' most dramatic roster reshaping in recent years. Sure, the year prior marked a wider exodus of names but none of them helped define Boston's vision and identity like Brown.

Through nine games, that's proven true on both ends of the floor. The Celtics have altered how they elect to deploy their offensive figureheads and spotlight George's defensive splendor — the product of Brown's absence (for better and worse) and George's arrival (for better and worse).

Without Brown, Boston does not sport a creator equipped to soak up mammoth on-ball volume beyond Tatum, who sports a career-high 36% usage rate (third league-wide, per Cleaning the Glass) and is averaging 27.9 points, 8.0 assists and 6.3 rebounds per game this season.

But the Celtics have not spiked Tatum's responsibilities by simply handing him the ball up top and demanding he stir more of their drink. His 27.2% on-ball rate is lower than in 2025-26 (28.3%) and 2024-25 (28.8%) while his 20.0 creation true shot attempts per 100 possessions (pick-and-rolls, isolation, post-ups) align with the previous two years as well (19.0, 19.4), per databallr.

Instead, they're leveraging his size, strength and fluidity to garner advantageous touches inside, dive to the rim around off-ball screens and attack tilted defenses on the move. A 33% rim frequency is his highest since 2022-23 and tied for the second-gaudiest mark of his career.

Tatum relishes contact these days and will bludgeon through any defender to finish or draw fouls. His .510 free-throw rate is obliterating a previous career-high of .399. Only Luka Doncic (102) and Giannis Antetokounmpo (83) have attempted more free throws than his 80 this season.

He is huge, strong and emboldened. The Celtics are wielding that to their benefit.

Plays like those emphasize another crucial detail in how Tatum's reached such towering usage: teammates are setting up him more often. Forty-seven percent of all his field goals are assisted, his highest rate since 2022-23, including 53% at the rim and 41% from midrange — also his highest rates since 2022-23, per Cleaning the Glass.

In contrast to their guard-sized contemporaries, wing-sized initiators often incur drawbacks with their burst, handle and flexibility. They are bigger and stronger, so they are slower and less adept maneuvering through tight windows. Yet they are bigger and stronger than their diminutive peers, traits that present their own upshots. As best they can, the Celtics are embracing the upshots to help mitigate the drawbacks while still bestowing Tatum cream-of-the-crop usage.

He is logging plenty of traditional creation reps, though, too. And when he is commandeering Boston's offense, he's looked spectacular as a facilitator. His 40.5% assist rate dwarfs his previous career-high of 26.5%.

His 16.7 potential assists per 100 are also a career high, besting his 15.8 in 2024-25. When he returned last season, he was mighty punctual and precise as a distributor, reliably dissecting opponents if they sent too many limbs into his orbit. That's continued this year.

Most notably, he's amended his playmaking patterns. Whereas he's typically erred for kickout passes to shooters against gap help or blitzes — partly because he's played with many below-the-rim and/or floor-spacing centers — he's prioritizing more interior reads this season and averaging a career-high 5.2 at-rim assists per 100 possessions (99th percentile).

That shatters last year's career-high of 3.0, which already trounced his then-career-high of 2.2 in 2024-25. Boston's rim frequency is 9.3% higher with him on the floor this season (per Cleaning the Glass), the result of his interior-oriented style and preferences as a passer.

Whether he's playing alongside Neemias Queta or Mitchell Robinson, he's utilizing his 84-inch acrobats to generate easy shots as the Celtics' primary initiator. His own height empowers him to disregard extra attention over the top and he's increasingly manipulating help defenders with his eyes to open up passes inside, particularly when George or Sam Hauser — Boston's most vaunted off-ball shooters — are stationed in the weak-side corner.

In totality, Tatum receiving newfound off-ball opportunities is a positive for his game. But a focus operating downhill and starting touches inside the arc has somewhat damaged his shot profile. According to Cleaning the Glass, 19% of his field goals are long midrange attempts (14 feet to the 3-point line) and 35% of his field goals fall under the broader midrange designation (beyond 4 feet to the 3-point line). The former is highest since 2018-19 and the latter is his highest since 2020-21.

Of course, there are a handful of voluminous scorers who flourish in the intermediate region. Tatum is not among them. He's shooting 39% from midrange and has only eclipsed 40% once in his decade-long NBA tenure.

Too routinely, he will catch the ball in the post or against a switch and squander the chance with an arduous pull-up 2, turnaround jumper or fadeaway. He's shooting 51.5% inside the arc, his worst mark since 2020-21, when he shot 52.4% — last year's abbreviated, post-injury stretch excluded.

An uptick in looks around the rim and from midrange has seen Tatum's 3-point rate dwindle to 30%, his lowest mark since 2018-19. And he's been anemic from deep thus far, shooting 28.6%. That should perk up as the sample swells and 51.5% on 2s isn't such a deviation from recent years that any long-range pulse, when paired with his premier foul-drawing emergence, should allow him to approximate or exceed the 60% true shooting he accrued from 2022-23 to 2024-25 (a very respectable 58% this year).

If Tatum has not suddenly devolved into a Thompson Twins-level outside shooter, the tweaks to his usage and passing upswing should set the stage for a career-best campaign offensively. And he is not the only Celtics wing embarking on a new offensive journey.

Boston recognizes it requires a substantial role for George offensively but cannot just hand him Brown's shoes and tell him to fit in them. He is nowhere near the shot creator or explosive driver of Brown but does have some advantages, such as size, versatile off-ball play and passing.

So, head coach Joe Mazzulla is constantly rotating George through various jobs to influence possessions without overtaxing him on the ball — and the early returns are excellent. He's averaging 19.6 points (58% true shooting), 5.5 rebounds, 4.5 assists and 2.4 steals while shooting 41.3% beyond the arc.

Despite a career-high 75% of his field goals coming via assists, he touts a 27.6% usage rate, his highest since 2022-23, and is averaging 9.1 creation true shooting attempts per 100, his lowest in the stat's database spanning back to 2013-14; his time of possession is also its lowest since 2017-18, per NBA.com. Similar to Tatum, Boston is granting George touches in favorable contexts by leaning on its blend of elite screening, decision-making and spacing around them.

As George has aged, he's lost much of his spunk as a driver. That physical decline has resigned him into many challenging midrange jumpers over defenders who he can no longer scoot past or chisel through to the rim. The Celtics don't want that as a basis of their offense, so they're implementing plenty of actions that manufacture advantages for George, whether it be off-ball screens to help him pressure the paint, forcing smaller defenders onto him or letting him catch the ball on the move to ease his creation burden.

His .508 3-point rate is a career high and he's hoisting a career-high 13.0 triples per 100 possessions. They're also utilizing him as a screener and roller more than any other team has in his career. According to databallr, his 3.7 finishing true shooting attempts (cuts, putbacks, rolls) per 100 is a career-high. Related: a 21% rim frequency is his highest since 2022-23. Boston's offense is helping George optimize his shot profile and he's taking his fewest share of midrange jumpers (31%) since 2011-12.

Reinvigorating George and Tatum as forces inside — and Tatum's playmaking pivot — feels like an intentional effort to replace Brown's driving prowess. The Celtics' 30.2% rim frequency is their highest since 2021-22 and ranks 16th league-wide, their highest standing since 2019-20. That 30.2% mark is loftier than anything they posted with Brown on the floor over his final three years in Boston, which isn't to frame Brown negatively but to underscore the Celtics' revamped approach offensively.

*Billy Beane voice* Boston is aiming to recreate Brown in the aggregate.

Tatum has commanded a heftier workload and is calling the rim home more often. George is the secondary creator, albeit a lesser one than Brown. Because Tatum's offensive role has expanded to help replace Brown, the Celtics are asking less of him defensively and calling upon George to fill that void and some of the void Brown left behind.

He's assuming Brown's former point-of-attack duties while doubling as an off-ball roamer who can guard bigs (Tatum's tasks) when Queta ignores a non-shooter to patrol the paint. He's third in the NBA in deflections per game (5.3) and fifth in steals (2.4), and owns a career-high STOP% of 4.7 (steals, blocks recovered and offensive fouls drawn per 100).

Just as Boston is highlighting Tatum's enormity on offense, it's doing the same with George on defense. His blend of size, screen navigation, instincts and off-ball disruption puts him in rare air defensively. He is huge and mobile. The Celtics are wielding that to their benefit.

Despite a 7-2 start with their leading men generally thriving, there is much to still sort out and navigate for the Celtics as the season further unfolds. Although I was higher than most on George's entire Sixers tenure and his pre-suspension performance in 2025-26, it is undeniable he played much better last year once returning from a 25-game layoff.

An offseason of recuperation offered a similar reprieve. And his scoring numbers since a 38-point barrage to open the year aren't as rosy: 17.0 points on 53.6% true shooting (37.3% from 3) over the past seven games. How will he fare on both ends as the grind of a long season mounts and transitions to a grueling playoff environment?

As splendid as Tatum's been offensively and wondrous as George has been defensively, I still believe Boston, to etch a deep postseason run, will need more of its franchise superstar defensively. His rim protection, general activity and rebounding on that end are at career-worst levels, the last of which is bleeding into Boston's 24th-ranked defensive rebounding rate (it's never finished worse than 11th in the Mazzulla era).

Perhaps, that is a conscious decision from him and the coaching staff, given his prolific offensive scope. But he is, ideally, capable of more. Will he dial it up at some point? Can he? It's a ton to ask, particularly because I don't believe this offensive setting has an off-ramp for him to effectively scale back.

And for as cohesive and cunning as the Celtics' offense is, I'm unsure of its playoff translation. They win on the margins with early flare screens, back-screens and drag screens that catch less-alert defenses off guard in the regular season — all details Tatum and George have ridden to early success.

When the stakes are raised in the postseason, countering schematic advantages is easier than countering talent advantages. The 2025-26 and 2026-27 Celtics have typically won because of the former, not the latter; their title team merged both components for dominance. This iteration lacks the ball-handling abundance and malleable center play the 2023-24 and 2024-25 squads enjoyed.

It is also possible a championship is not Boston's utmost goal or one the organization internally deems plausible this year. Building a team unable to do so during its All-NBA superstar's prime may warrant criticism but is a separate conversation.

This is Tatum's first full year back from a torn Achilles. George, while still very good, is 36 years old and not an All-Star-caliber player anymore. His lucrative contract will be an expiring deal next summer. Derrick White, while a pillar of Boston's prosperous last half-decade, is 32 and potentially trending downward.

Maybe, the Celtics are content to see what this Tatum-led group can achieve before parlaying George's expiring deal and their wealth of first-round picks into a better, younger upgrade over the offseason. Maybe, they continue piling up wins and feel compelled to make a splash at this season's deadline.

Regardless, this team is good but different than years past and still spearheaded by Tatum — who, for the first time in his career, is flanked by a new running mate on the wing.

All stats accurate prior to games played on Nov. 8.

#2892. Millions of dollars poured into prediction markets on election night, trading on Democratic control of Congress and Texas governor's race

More than 87% of all election markets across Kalshi and Polymarket gave the right candidate at least a 90% chance of winning their race 24 hours before election night, a CNBC analysis found.

Election night showed how traders reacted to real-time midterm results, as trading activity surged and market prices shifted to Democrats after results from Indiana began rolling in before 7 p.m. on Nov. 3. Many midterm races were uncompetitive, which boosted the overall hit rate on prediction markets.

"I'd say prediction markets did pretty well," said Koleman Strumpf, a professor of economics at Wake Forest University. "But they didn't outperform the best," he added, referring to traditional forecast polls that tracked results that night.

Odds and gambling platforms do not use methodologies used by traditional political polling, and therefore are not substitutes for political polls.

Prediction markets saw big trades on election night.

After 8 p.m. on Nov. 3, at least $1 million was traded on a Kalshi market that incumbent Gov. Greg Abbott would be reelected. Abbott eventually lost to Democrat Gina Hinojosa by more than 700,000 votes, according to the Associated Press. A little after 11 p.m. on election night, more than $1.4 million was traded on Kalshi that Republicans would not secure control of the House of Representatives.

Following the midterms, both Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan and Kalshi CEO Tarek Mansour were on the social media platform X crediting prediction markets for correctly predicting races. Nate Silver, a political forecaster and founder of the Silver Bulletin newsletter, disputed the claim, arguing forecast models influenced prediction market movement. Silver joined Polymarket as an advisor in 2024.

"Prediction market probabilities strongly converged toward [our models] in individual Senate and gubernatorial races," Silver wrote on Silver Bulletin. "I was tracking this stuff closely, precisely because I envisioned that we might wind up in this sort of debate where prediction markets are low-key stealing valor from the models."

Prediction market volume on midterm election-related contracts across Kalshi and Polymarket more than tripled from the last week of October into the midterms week, according to a CNBC analysis of data published on Predictions.com. The data did not include combo markets, which bundle multiple contracts and pay out only if every leg resolves in the trader's favor.

Still, volume on midterm contracts pales in comparison with sports volume, which remains larger than all other categories.

Kalshi exceeded $3 billion in total midterm contract volume, according to the platform's website, which also includes volume for contracts on mayoral races. Last week, Polymarket's two exchanges reported nearly $700 million in volume for midterm contracts.

While prediction markets broadly anticipated Democratic control of Congress, the blue tsunami is expected to bite the industry in other ways.

Three days after the midterms, Sen. Adam Schiff, D-Calif, criticized the federal regulator responsible for the platforms, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, for allowing sports contracts.

"Sports prediction markets are sports bets. By effectively requiring prediction markets to offer contracts in all 50 states, even on Tribal lands, the CFTC is once again violating state law and Tribal sovereignty," Schiff wrote on X.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2891. Harry Kane reflects on emotional Ballon d’Or triumph and reveals secret behind his success

The Bayern Munich striker admitted he had never experienced such pressure before, despite the numerous high-profile matches he has played throughout his career, as he reflected on the moment his name was announced as the winner.

“I went through every possible emotion. I was really nervous. I had never felt that kind of pressure before, even after all the football matches I’ve played, because this time, the outcome wasn’t really in my hands,” Kane explained in an interview with Téléfoot.

“When I walked onto the stage, I felt an incredible sense of relief. My heart rate was really high. I was almost frozen when I heard my name. Everything around me just stopped, like in a movie. It was a very nice feeling.”

Kane also discussed the message he shared following his triumph, writing: ‘Work hard in silence. Let success be your noise.’

The England captain revealed that the quote has resonated with him for several years, reflecting the mentality that has helped him reach the highest level of the game.

“It’s something I’ve come across many years ago, when I was younger. I think it’s just been something that suits me as a person,” he said.

The 33-year-old highlighted the importance of the work that supporters rarely get to see, pointing towards additional treatment, ice baths and finishing sessions after training as examples of the dedication that has helped him continually improve.

“It’s almost what I enjoy the most sometimes. When I look back through my journey, when things looked not so good, I think it’s what makes the journey so special,” Kane added.

Having initially admitted that winning football’s most prestigious individual award was beyond his expectations, Kane explained how his ambitions gradually developed as his career progressed.

“I simply wanted to become a good footballer, and then I gradually improved over time. As my career progressed, I became more interested in the Ballon d’Or. I started thinking, ‘Why not?’ But winning this award is beyond anything I could have imagined.”

Kane also credited his move to Munich with helping him reach another level, insisting that he is currently playing the best football of his career.

“Since arriving in Munich, I’ve taken another step forward. Today, I’m in the best form of my life. I hope to give my very best until the end,” he concluded.

With his individual achievements now matching his outstanding performances on the pitch, Kane’s comments underline the mentality and dedication that have helped him reach the pinnacle of world football.

#2889. Bucks Reacts Survey: Did Milwaukee win the Giannis trade?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NBA. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bucks fans and fans across the country.

The old adage proclaims that the team that gets the best player wins the trade. By that logic, the Miami Heat are the clear-cut winners in the trade that sent Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bobby Portis to the Heat in exchange for Tyler Herro, Jaime Jaquez Jr., Kel'el Ware, Kasparas Jakučionis, and picks, including the 13th-overall selection in this year's draft, which ended up being Nate Ament. But is it all that simple?

Miami traded for Giannis on the basis of winning now. Three weeks into the season, that's not really happening, with the Heat going 4-6 and sitting eighth in the Eastern Conference standings (moving up after a narrow win over the Sacramento Kings last night). Moreover, the Heat were billed as a defensive-juggernaut-to-be, yet that reality couldn't be further from the truth. At this point of the season, the Heat rank 26th in defensive rating (116.8), 27th in opponent points per game (121.1 PPG), and 28th in opponent field goal percentage (48.8%). Their defensive struggles aren't lost on head coach extraordinaire Erik Spoelstra, who said as much himself:

We're losing games because of our defense. That has to be our 1st, 2nd, and 3rd priorities. Everything else we'll start to make some progress.

Others, including ESPN's Brian Windhorst and Tim MacMahon, have noticed too:

Of course, the Milwaukee Bucks have their own problems—plenty of 'em, including being too nice—and, despite being in most of their games thus far, find themselves sitting just 1-9 and in last place.

Individually, Giannis has put up similar numbers to what he did for Milwaukee: 27.5 PPG, 12.8 RPG, 5.3 APG, 1.0 SPG, and 1.1 BPG. However, he's suddenly copping a lot more criticism in the media—a "perk" for requesting a trade to a big market, perhaps. Most notably, former coach Doc Rivers spoke about how Giannis is "not easy" to coach, while former player Nick Young was downright abusive of Giannis on Gil's Arena. Manner aside, both of these point to game-style issues that have long been known, at least around here. Portis, meanwhile, is struggling to find his groove in South Beach, averaging just 7.0 PPG and 6.3 RPG on .371/.325/.714 shooting splits.

Closer to home, the Bucks' recruits have been a mixed bag. Herro has been great, air-frying his way to 21.4 PPG, 4.6 RPG, and 4.3 APG and providing rare veteran stability for a young team trying to figure things out (including how to go from competing to winning). Jakučionis has shown strong glimpses, as has Ament, who is likely ahead of the curve many projected for him. Jaquez has also been a positive, arguably playing the best ball of his career, although his long-term tenure on the team is anything but guaranteed having not come to terms on a rookie-scale extension. Then there's Ware, who many saw as the prize acquisition and a potential breakout candidate, but who has been underwhelming to say the least, exhibiting many of the concerning habits that came to light during his time in Miami—and who seems to be finding a home in Taylor Jenkins' doghouse.

For Miami, the goal of the trade is clear: win a championship. Pat Riley owned this, saying "We are here to win. Giannis wants to win. Our plan is to go for it right from the beginning." Giannis did too:

This puts a premium on the present. But if the Heat aren't contending this year, then there's a helluva lot of pressure to win next year—when Giannis will be turning 33—otherwise it might just be too late. For at least one fan, this pressure, not to mention the impending contract pressure when Giannis' extension options become more lucrative in January, is not missed.

For Milwaukee, the play is obviously long term. Bereft of many (any?) real assets prior to the trade, this was a chance for the Bucks to build anew. And, fingers and toes—and anything else you can—crossed that the Bucks aren't punished for the Gary Trent Jr. deal, they are in a better position to build than they would have been rehashing the same plan as yesteryear. Right?

So, three-or-so weeks into the season, now that we've seen how the acquisitions and teams look, how do you evaluate the trade?

Let us know your thoughts by voting below—and don't forget to explain your reasons in the comments.

#2885. Jaguars land DT Jordan Davis in blockbuster deal with Eagles, marking latest trade ahead of deadline

The Jacksonville Jaguars are going all in.

The Jaguars struck a deal to land Philadelphia Eagles defensive tackle Jordan Davis on Monday afternoon, just a day ahead of the NFL trade deadline, the team announced. In return, the Eagles are getting multiple draft picks, including a first-round selection for next April.

The deal is the third the Jaguars made on Monday to add a defensive player. They acquired safety Jessie Bates III in a trade with the Atlanta Falcons, and also picked up defensive tackle Davon Godchaux in a trade with the New Orleans Saints.

Davis is in his fifth season in the league. He racked up a career-high 72 total tackles and 4.5 sacks during the 2025 campaign, and has 31 total tackles and 1.5 sacks through nine games this season. The 26-year-old, whom the Eagles selected with the No. 13 overall pick in 2022 out of Georgia, signed a three-year, $78 million extension with the Eagles in March.

"We are extremely grateful to Jordan for his contributions to our team on the field and in the Philadelphia community since we drafted him five years ago. He has been a key contributor to our team culture and on-field success, which included two Super Bowl appearances and a world championship in 2024," the Eagles said in a statement.

"His growth and development as both a player and a leader are a testament to his hard work and character. We wish Jordan nothing but continued success."

Philadelphia is on a seven-game skid after Sunday's 19-11 loss to the New York Giants. The Eagles (2-7) are last in the NFC East and are all but out of the playoff race this season — especially with quarterback Jalen Hurts sidelined due to blood clots. The latest move gives the Eagles an additional first-round pick in next year's draft and a second-round pick in 2029.

They now have a combined 10 picks in the first two rounds of the next three drafts, including two in the first round in both 2027 and 2028. That should give the organization plenty of room to regroup after this season ends.

Meanwhile, Jacksonville clearly feels like it has a roster capable of making a Super Bowl push, and Monday's moves only bolster that feeling. The three additions will complement an already tough defense that has allowed just 15.1 points per game this season — which is the second-best output in the league behind only the Minnesota Vikings. The Jags sit at 6-2 coming out of Week 9, after they beat the Baltimore Ravens 33-10 for their fifth win in the past six outings.

Jacksonville no longer has a first- or second-round draft pick in 2027 after Monday's transactions, though the Jaguars still hold their first-round picks in 2028 and 2029.

The Jaguars have been to the playoffs only twice in the past nine seasons, and they fell in the wild-card round in 2025, the first under head coach Liam Coen's watch. While Monday's transactions were undoubtedly costly for the franchise, the Jaguars feel ready to go for it and make a legitimate postseason run. Whether that is enough for them to run the table in the AFC and make what would be the team's first Super Bowl remains to be seen.

Fantasy basketball managers who patiently waited for Kon Knueppel's debut were quickly disappointed after he lasted less than 10 minutes before aggravating his hamstring injury. Then, Keyonte George left Sunday's contest after suffering an ankle injury.

Dunn will eventually be traded, but until then, make sure he's not on waivers. He is chipping in across the board, averaging 8.5 points, 4.0 rebounds, 4.5 assists, 2.8 steals and 0.6 blocks in 30.4 minutes. He's recorded multiple steals in six of eight games. The Clippers' backcourt has underwhelmed outside of Dunn, so the minutes will continue to be there. He's posting 31 FPPG in High Score and will be worth streaming (at minimum) on Tuesday at Phoenix. He's been a top-100 player in 9-cat through four weeks and is a strong source of points, 3s and steals with a low turnover rate.

As a starter, he's averaging 13.0 points, 8.3 rebounds and 3.5 assists. His shooting has been rough (42.6% from the field), so his production comes from his ability to generate counting stats in the core categories. With Knueppel out at least four weeks, and Brandon Miller and Dennis Schröder ruled out for Monday's clash against the Cavs, Allen will see more shot volume and usage to start the week. The latter two may not be back by Thursday, so it could be a busy week for the nine-year vet. Charlotte plays on Monday, Thursday at Phoenix and Sunday at the Clippers. #2881. A massive trade in Nvidia just went off – here are the details

The biggest options trade on Nvidia stock Monday seems to think the controversy swirling around the chip leader and its embattled CEO is creating a buying opportunity.In a trade about an hour after the opening bell that involved more than $100 million in options premium, someone exchanged an existing call spread for a more bullish position that captures the next five months of trading in Nvidia.The core position was the purchase of 52,000 195-strike calls expiring Feb. 19 worth about $57 million – contracts the trader opened for a new long position while simultaneously selling the same number of 245-strike calls for the same expiry, lowering the cost of the trade by $10 million. The net position, a bullish call spread, needs Nvidia to trade above around $206 by expiry, or about 15% higher, to be profitable. If Nvidia stock reaches the short, 245-strike call, it would represent a nearly 40% rally and return the stock to its all-time high of $243, that was reached on October 6th.The trade was likely part of a "roll" down from an existing 210/255-strike call spread worth about $42 million that was closed out, according to SpotGamma analysis.

"The person got more aggressive rolling this spread down to lower strikes to get more upside exposure by getting higher delta," SpotGamma founder Brent Kochuba said by phone. "They're trading the call spread here because the vol is expensive going into earnings. It's a significant increase in upside delta exposure."Delta is options parlance for describing how quickly an options price will react to a change in the underlying stock price. The trader's updated position, taken at lower strikes, will make money faster if Nvidia rallies. In other words, it's a higher conviction trade that the chipmaker's stock will soon rebound. The 195-strike calls they bought for $57 million was the single biggest options trade on the tape Monday, according to SpotGamma. Of the top 20 options trades by dollar-amount, 12 were Nvidia, the data show.Flows around the stock generally leaned bullish by midday, with 57% of options volume tied to calls and more than $660 million of call premium likely initiated by call-buyers, SpotGamma data show. The most popular single contract by volume was the 180-strike call expiring Monday, that needs Nvidia to recover 1.2% by the bell to break even.

Nacua is dealing with sprains to both his PCL and MCL and is also dealing with turf toe, McVay said.

Nacua carried the Rams to a 20-13 victory over the Washington Commanders on Sunday, Nov. 8. He had 14 catches for 150 yards and a touchdown and had 19 targets, along with a pair of rushing attempts (for 20 yards). He was targeted 24 times in the Rams' overtime loss to the Buffalo Bills on Oct. 12.

#2876. Giannis Antetokounmpo says he's 'speechless' over criticism from former coach Doc Rivers: 'That's insane'

Miami Heat star Giannis Antetokounmpo was "speechless" over criticism from his former Milwaukee Bucks coach Doc Rivers.

Rivers made those comments during an appearance on "The Bill Simmons Podcast," which was posted Thursday. During the episode, Rivers said Antetokounmpo wanted to leave the Bucks but wouldn't ask for a trade so he could continue being viewed as the good guy.

Rivers later added that Antetokounmpo "doesn't necessarily have this super IQ" and that he "really has one way: Downhill, force, force."

When asked about those comments Sunday, Antetokounmpo initially said he was "speechless" before trying to brush them aside, per ESPN.

"First of all, he's not my coach," Antetokounmpo told reporters after Miami's 114-108 victory over the Sacramento Kings. "My coach is Coach Spo [Erik Spoelstra], but [Rivers] is a guy — a coach — that I've worked with in the past. I'm speechless."

"You got to go ask him why he thought that," Antetokounmpo said. "That's on him, that's not on me. I just got to focus on my team and what I got to do and how I can be better and how can I help the team."

Later on, however, Antetokounmpo implied Rivers was fueled by "bitterness and pettiness."

"But God, that's crazy. Never let bitterness or pettiness get in front of your goals and dreams. For every athlete out there, never let that — that's insane. But you got to go ask him why he said what he said. Not me."

Heat coach Erik Spoelstra was also asked about Rivers' comments. The Heat coach declined to feed into them, saying he liked and respected Rivers.

Rivers and Antetokounmpo worked together for two-and-a-half years in Milwaukee. While the team made the playoffs in two of those seasons, it disappointed, falling in the first round in both years.

Antetokounmpo performed extremely well in his first two years under Rivers, averaging 30.4 points per game. The star big man spent a large stretch of last season injured.

The Bucks responded to the down year by jettisoning both men. Rivers stepped down from his role as head coach and Antetokounmpo was traded to the Heat. Rivers opted to return to the broadcast booth for the 2026-27 NBA season. Antetokounmpo is averaging 27.5 points and 12.8 rebounds in his first season with the Heat.

#2873. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#2869. The Puka Nacua injury update is not good

The Los Angeles Rams will be without their biggest offensive weapon for at least the next four games. Wide receiver Puka Nacua is headed to injured reserve after suffering MCL and PCL injuries to his knee, creating a significant challenge for Sean McVay and an offense that has consistently relied on his production.

Nacua suffered the injury during Sunday's 20-13 victory over the Washington Commanders. The injury occurred on the Rams' final offensive possession when Nacua took a hard hit to his knee on a jet sweep. What initially appeared to be a concerning situation has now resulted in a trip to injured reserve and an extended absence.

The timing is particularly unfortunate for Los Angeles. Nacua was coming off an outstanding performance against Washington, recording 14 receptions for 150 yards and a touchdown. He has been Matthew Stafford's most dependable target and arguably the engine behind the Rams' passing attack.

Nacua has 70 catches for 715 yards and four touchdowns, averaging 102.1 yards per game. He led the NFL with 129 catches and 107.2 yards per game in 2025. Nacua's production is rare and offenses rarely rely on one player as much as L.A. has put on their number one receiver over the last 3.5 seasons.

With Nacua unavailable for the next four games, the Rams will need their remaining offensive playmakers to shoulder a much larger workload. Davante Adams is also on injured reserve, making the situation even more difficult for Stafford.

Los Angeles still has postseason aspirations, but losing Nacua for the next month puts additional pressure on McVay to find offensive production elsewhere.

#2867. Uli Hoeneß raises concerns over Jamal Musiala’s future at Bayern Munich

Speaking today, Hoeneß revealed that Musiala is dealing with an illness that cannot simply be cured, although medication can help manage the situation.

“With Musiala, the situation is completely different. We’re in a dilemma there. He has an illness that cannot simply be cured, but can be managed to some extent with medication,” Hoeneß explained, as reported by BR24.

The Bayern figure also left the door open over whether the 23-year-old will ever rediscover the form that previously made him one of Europe’s most exciting attacking talents.

“Whether he will ever regain the level of performance he once had remains uncertain,” Hoeneß added.

Musiala’s situation also presents a difficult contractual dilemma for Bayern, with Hoeneß highlighting that the illness is not covered by the insurance provisions in his contract.

He explained that the midfielder continues to receive his full salary, despite the uncertainty surrounding his ability to return to his previous performances.

“Just imagine if FC Bayern were now to question his contract and say: ‘What he is capable of delivering now is no longer what was stipulated in the contract’,” Hoeneß remarked.

The comments offer a concerning insight into the uncertainty surrounding Musiala’s situation, particularly given his importance to Bayern’s long-term sporting plans.

The Germany international has endured a difficult period, and while Bayern will continue to support him through his recovery, Hoeneß’s latest remarks suggest there are still significant question marks over what the future holds for the attacking midfielder.

#2866. The story behind Mike Conley’s first Celtics start: “I thought they were joking”

MILWAUKEE — Mike Conley didn't hear from his coach that he was starting in Sunday's game.

He heard it from the very Celtics teammates who picked him.

The 39-year-old point guard, in his 20th NBA season, got his first start of the year against the Milwaukee Bucks after Joe Mazzulla handed the decision to Jayson Tatum and Derrick White, Conley exclusively told CelticsBlog late Sunday night outside the visiting locker room.

The two Celtics veterans decided that Conley, who had barely played through Boston's first 9 games of the season, had earned the opportunity with his strong fourth-quarter play against the Charlotte Hornets.

"I heard it from D-White and JT," Conley said after the team's 117-97 win over the Bucks. "They were like, 'Hey, you're starting in Milwaukee.' I was like, 'What?'"

Prior to Sunday, Conley had played a total of 22 minutes all season.

So when Tatum and White approached him after Boston's blowout win over the Hornets on Friday, he didn't take them seriously.

"They looked at me after the Charlotte game. They're like, 'So you want to start next game?'" Conley said. "I'm just like, 'What are you talking about?' It's not really on my radar at the moment, so I thought they were joking, honestly."

Conley had played well in his fourth-quarter stint against the Hornets, and Mazzulla and the team appreciated his ball pressure.

"I thought Mike did a great job getting us off to a [good] start in the fourth quarter against Charlotte with his ball pressure, with his defensive activity for us," Mazzulla said. "And I wanted that same thing to start the game."

But Mazzulla, who has openly stressed player empowerment all season long, wanted to give the players a chance to decide.

On Sunday morning, Mazzulla walked into the Celtics' team meeting and asked the room who should start; Jordan Walsh started in 6 of the Celtics' first 9 games, while Hugo Gonzalez got the chance in three of them.

"He was like, 'So who's starting?'" Conley recalled. "He left it up to his players. And JT is like, 'Mike, me…,' and gave the starting lineup. I'm like, it's real."

That meant Conley, White, Tatum, Paul George, and Neemias Queta started against the Bucks.

Conley said he wasn't nervous — he's started for most of his career, after all —but it was his first time sharing the floor with Tatum and George, which he thoroughly enjoyed.

"It was the first time literally stepping on the court with them," he said. "It was a lot of fun, just to get out there and get some run and play in a competitive atmosphere."

The Celtics didn't get off to a great start on Sunday night; Bucks forward Kyle Kuzma opened 6-of-10 for 16 first-quarter points, and the Celtics trailed by as many as 14 in the opening frame.

But Mazzulla didn't attribute the slow start to Conley's play.

"I thought his minutes were great," Mazzulla said, adding that the Celtics missed several layups that helped put them in the hole.

Despite the slow start, the Celtics responded with a 33-18 second quarter, headlined by 16 points from a scorching-hot Paul George. They ultimately pulled away for a 117-97 win, improving to an 8-2 record on the season.

Postgame, Mazzulla downplayed the importance of who starts, as he has throughout much of his tenure as head coach.

"At the end of the day, the starting lineup is one piece of the 15 to 20 lineups that we play in the game," Mazzulla said. "All the lineups matter."

In 20 seasons in the NBA, Conley said, he had never seen players get a say in who starts or take on the kinds of off-court responsibilities that Celtics players have this season.

"This is the first place," he said. "Our players do scouts. We do film sessions. We have so much empowerment from Joe, and I think it's just a unique atmosphere and culture built around that. So, that's just another extension of it."

Conley said the team has handled the constantly changing lineups well because everyone keeps the team's success front and center.

"Everybody, Jordan Walsh to Hugo, whoever started prior games, nobody cares," he said. "Everybody just goes out there and supports each other, and that's cool."

While all the players have had the chance to take on more significant responsibilities in film and scouting, perhaps no player has taken on more than Conley.

"I've got a lot to give, with my experience and things I've been through, and how I see and read the game maybe differently than some other players," Conley said. "So I try to give that to as many guys as I can, and try to prepare them and give them options, and have them see things through my eyes a couple times throughout the games. And to see them go out there and execute is pretty cool. And I know Joe supports me in that, so he gives me the freedom to pull a guy to the side, or tell him to do this or that, or change something up, or even tell Joe to do something different. So it's a unique, unique spot, and then also being able to play too — it's pretty cool."

During training camp, Conley told CelticsBlog that he knew his on-court role would be inconsistent, and that some nights he'd play and some nights he wouldn't.

"It's part of the job at this stage of my career," he told CelticsBlog at training camp in Charleston. "I understand it. I understand there's going to be a lot of times where I don't play. There's going to be some times where I'm needed. There are other times where I'm just needed to be the voice for a big stretch of the season. Being comfortable with that, and being able to own that, is my role and my job."

What has surprised him, however, is that the Stay-Ready bench role has actually made him expend more energy, not less.

"You're not just relaxing and chilling," he said. "You're actually working harder than you probably ever have, because you never know when your number is going to be called. Your wheels have to be turning, your brain has to be on."

Mazzulla said he's been impressed with Conley's minutes on the court.

"There's been times where he just comes in off the bench, he gets a shot right away," he said. "His defensive presence has been great. So I think that's just as important, because he still takes pride in playing. That's great to see at this stage of his career."

Still, Conley's off-court impact might be his most profound contribution to the team. On Sunday, Mazzulla called Conley "an extension of the coaching staff", and said he's made a concerted effort to learn from him.

"He has 20 years of experience, and I'm going to pick his brain just as much as the players are," Mazzulla said. "He's played for great coaches, had great teammates. We all just need to be able to take from his wisdom."

Conley has run film sessions and handled part of the scouting and defensive game-planning for the Celtics' game against the Orlando Magic last week, in which they held the Magic to just 97 points. When he signed in Boston, he knew he'd be expected to help lead in the locker room.

"I knew that I'd obviously be a voice and a vet leader and all that stuff," Conley said. "But to the extent of running film sessions, picking out clips, stuff like that — I didn't know that was going to be part of the job description."

"It's something I think is unique and really cool about being here," he said. "And, I welcome it the rest of the year."

Asked whether this season's unique experience has pointed toward coaching, Conley laughed.

"I always tell them, I don't know if I can coach the young guys, man. Y'all run me in circles," he said. "But I'll never shut the door on it."

For now, he's enjoying the unique opportunity to be a key mind on one of the NBA's top teams and a rare active player who can take on real coaching responsibilities.

"Hopefully I continue to get better at it," Conley said, "and continue to bridge that gap of player and coach and teammate."

#2864. NFL reinstates WR Brandon Aiyuk from reserve/left squad list

Brandon Aiyuk is back, and the ball is in the 49ers' court.

The NFL reinstated the 49ers receiver from the reserve/left squad list on Monday. The league also gave the 49ers a two-week roster exemption, giving the team a window for making a decision about what to do with Aiyuk.

The 49ers now have an immediate decision to make about Aiyuk. If he's on the roster on Tuesday at 4:00 p.m. ET, he's owed a game check for the week.

Based on the Player Personnel Manual, placement on the reserve/left squad list apparently tolled his 2025 salary. That means he'll return to the $1.17 million base salary from 2025, with the 2026 salary of $26.015 million delayed until 2027.

It gives the 49ers a window to trade Aiyuk, with the new team getting him for the rest of the season at only $585,000 in non-guaranteed pay.

The 49ers can carry Aiyuk as an extra man on the roster until Monday, November 23.

It's unclear, currently, whether Aiyuk agreed to refund past bonus money to the 49ers in order to get the league to reinstate him. If the 49ers trade or release Aiyuk, the 49ers lose the ability to get money back from Aiyuk.

On Monday, the Green Bay Packers worked out six players, all either quarterbacks or tight ends. In theory, they have space to add, at least until running back Chris Brooks and edge defender Micah Parsons return to the 53-man roster, which could displace someone to the practice squad.

Carter turned down college scholarships to power programs like Indiana, Mississippi, Pittsburgh, Rutgers, West Virginia and Wisconsin to start his college football career at Toledo, where he posted a 5-5 record before transferring to South Alabama for his final two seasons of eligibility. In the Sun Belt, he was named an all-conference player, earning him an opportunity with the Las Vegas Raiders as an undrafted free agent in 2024.

He's also had previous experience with the San Francisco 49ers and Jacksonville Jaguars, whose head coaches come from the same coaching tree as head coach Matt LaFleur. So far, Carter — the son of long-time defensive coordinator Gus Bradley — has yet to throw a regular-season pass.

This is Carter's second workout since he was waived with an injury settlement at the cutdown deadline. His other workout came with the Arizona Cardinals, coached by LaFleur's brother.

Morton was previously a Green Bay Packer, as he signed with the team as an undrafted free agent back in 2020. He was cut during the preseason, but he eventually signed a reserve-futures deal with the Indianapolis Colts for the 2021 season, and he was waived again during the preseason.

Since his stints in the NFL, he's gone to play with the Hamilton Tiger-Cats (CFL), Birmingham Stallions (USFL/UFL), Southwest Kansas Storm (CIF), Houston Roughnecks (UFL) and Columbus Aviators (UFL).

Davidson is a former college punter who received all-conference honors at the Division II level before also contributing as a tight end later in his Central Missouri career. Fletcher is a rookie undrafted free agent who moved from defensive end to the offensive side of the ball in 2024 while at Rutgers. He was not a highly touted UDFA in the 2026 class, signing for just $3,000 guaranteed with the Tampa Bay Buccaneers — the lowest rate of any tight end who received any guarantees in this class.

He was waived by the Buccaneers at the cutdown deadline, but he later spent about a month with the Indianapolis Colts' practice squad. Since being let go by the Colts, he also worked out with the Seattle Seahawks.

Kuntz is a former draft pick who had a lot of hype coming into the league as a raw project due to his athleticism. He originally was a major recruit for Penn State before transferring to Old Dominion. He's since kicked around on the New York Jets, Miami Dolphins, Las Vegas Raiders and Dallas Cowboys, but has only played two regular-season games — most recently in 2024 — with 10 of his 13 snaps on special teams.

Since being let go by the Cowboys at the cutdown deadline, he's also had a workout with the Indianapolis Colts.

Rogers was a former super recruit who was a one-year starter for the TCU Horned Frogs in 2025. In undrafted free agency, he signed with the Dallas Cowboys for $200,000 guaranteed, the fifth-highest mark among 28 UDFA tight ends. For perspective, the Packers signed their UDFA tight end, R.J. Maryland, for $15,000 guaranteed.

In the preseason, Rogers sprained his MCL, leading to him being waived with an injured designation at the cutdown deadline. Because he wasn't claimed, he reverted back to the Cowboys' injured reserve, where he was waived with an injury settlement in late October.

This appears to be Rodgers' first workout since being let go by Dallas.
