#2857. Jessie Bates Facing Wild Scenario After His Trade to The Jaguars

The Jacksonville Jaguars landed a big one just a day before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline. On Monday, reports surfaced that the Jaguars are acquiring Pro Bowl defensive back Jessie Bates III from the Atlanta Falcons.

Trade: Falcons are sending former Pro Bowl safety Jessie Bates to the Jacksonville Jaguars in exchange for a 2027 2nd-round pick and a 2028 5th-round pick, sources tell ESPN.

With the trade, the Jags are adding depth and experience to their secondary that some felt the team needs to address before the trade deadline.

The 29-year-old Bates spent three-plus seasons with the Falcons, who signed him to a 4-year, $64 million contract in 2023. Before that, he played for five seasons with the Cincinnati Bengals, who selected him in the second round of the 2018 NFL Draft.

Atlanta free safety Bates (3) paces between plays in the fourth quarter of the NFL International Week 9 game between the Atlanta Falcons and the Cincinnati Bengals at Bernabéu Stadium in Madrid on Sunday, Nov. 8, 2026. The Bengals won the international matchup, 34-17.

It remains to be seen just how big of an impact Bates can have on Jacksonville's stop unit. But it's also worth noting that Bates could see no extra week of rest for the entire 2026 NFL season.

"Jessie Bates still hasn't had a bye this season and is now joining a Jaguars team that already had theirs. If Bates suits up right away, there's a scenario where he plays 18 games this season," Schefter noted in another post.

The Falcons' bye is in Week 11, while the Jaguars already had theirs in Week 7, so this is a timeline coincidence for Bates, who has recorded at least an interception in each of his seasons so far in the NFL.

#2855. Fantasy Basketball High Score Perfect Lineup: Victor Wembanyama's historic game leads the way in Week 3

We're approaching the one-month mark of the 2026-27 NBA regular season, and it's been a rough go for fantasy basketball managers trying to dodge injuries. Case in point: Lakers superstar Luka Dončić sat out Sunday's game but still managed to deliver earlier in the week (more on that below).

But if you're back here, you're enjoying your second full season of Yahoo High Score fantasy basketball. As always, the top point total from each player each week is what counts toward your final goal. Let's take a look at the perfect lineup from Week 3 and how the top performers fared. One of the players had a historic performance.

Luka Dončić: As was mentioned above, Dončić wasn't able to go on Sunday against the Pistons due to lower-back tightness. It's likely not anything to get concerned about, more just giving the MVP frontrunner a rest day to end the week. But to start the week, Dončić produced his usual gaudy stat line, scoring 42 points with 13 assists, 7 rebounds and 2 steals to make this list. If there was a poster child for High Score fantasy, it is Dončić.

Keyonte George: The one non-superstar name on this list is the Jazz guard, who exploded for 39 points, 9 assists, 2 rebounds and 5 stocks against the Pelicans. Utah had struggled in back-to-back games prior to facing New Orleans, which will be an issue for George managers in some weeks. But, again, that's the beauty of High Score — all it takes is one. He did leave Sunday's contest with an ankle injury, which is something to monitor.

Victor Wembanyama: The Spurs' big man made fantasy basketball history by tying Bam Adebayo for the highest point total in High Score with 110. Adebayo's legendary 83-point game from last season got it done. But Wemby's stat line was a bit wilder: 52 points, 24 rebounds, 8 blocks, 2 assists and 2 steals. He almost had a 52-24-10 triple-double with blocks. We may not see another fantasy output like this all season.

Nikola Jokić: The Joker is going to pop up plenty on this list throughout the season, though he has been off to a slow start in 2026-27. His High Score on the year so far is 85 points, which is low by his lofty standards. The perennial MVP candidate is also dealing with an elbow injury that may hold him out of Monday's contest to start Week 4. Not good. But Jokić also collected his third triple-double of the season with a 32-10-11 line against the Raptors.

Giannis Antetokounmpo: The Greek Freak has been one of the more steady options in fantasy basketball so far this season. He's recorded a double-double in seven games, including two triple-doubles. The Heat split an L.A. road trip against the Lakers and Clippers, but Antetokounmpo posted 65+ fantasy points in each contest. Managers of the star are foaming at the mouth looking at the Week 4 schedule — at Sacramento, at Utah — to start things off.

Shai Gilgeous-Alexander: Things have not gone as planned for the Thunder to start the campaign. Injuries keep piling up with Jalen Williams, Chet Holmgren and Cason Wallace all out long term. Now, Jaylin Williams is on the mend, so the team traded for Nique Clifford via the Kings. Anyway, SGA has been a relatively strong fantasy asset per usual, averaging close to 30 points per game with decent category coverage. If anything, the injuries may benefit Gilgeous-Alexander from a fantasy perspective. It means OKC is in more close games and the two-time MVP will need to continue to step up each game.

#2854. Mariners look forward and back in current coaching staff hires

The Mariners made the hires of bench coach Tanner Swanson and hitting coach CJ Gillman official today, continuing a theme that started when the hiring of manager Jason Kanzler was officially announced last week: the youth movement is coming back to Seattle.

In Dipoto's first season, the Mariners trended towards conservative hires or retaining coaches who had already been in the organization. Dipoto brought over several people he'd worked with during his time with the Angels – Mike Micucci, Ethan Katz, and Zac Livingston – and retained most other staff members rather than cleaning house organizationally from top to bottom.

But by 2016, Dipoto and then-Director of Player Development Andy McKay were ready to take some bigger swings. One of the biggest was hiring a 32-year-old Mitch Canham to manage their A-ball affiliate, the Clinton LumberKings. At the time, Canham was well-known to Northwest baseball fans as a standout star at Lake Stevens high school and then Oregon State, but an unknown quantity in the broader coaching world, lacking any official managerial experience before being handed the reins at a full-season affiliate.

Canham's hiring was part of an organizational philosophy shift that emphasized quality of leadership over depth of experience – as McKay put it, "better people make better baseball players", which starts with them being led by the best people available. Joining Canham in Clinton was pitching coach Pete Woodworth, who had more official coaching experience but was just 28 years old at the time.

Those hires sparked an influx of young coaching talent, often from non-traditional coaching backgrounds, into the Mariners organization. The next few years would see the hiring of at the time relatively unknown names, many of whom who have since moved on from Seattle to build impressive coaching resumes elsewhere: Tony Arnerich, Carson Vitale, Hugh Quattlebaum, Max Weiner, Jarret DeHart, Rob Marcello, Sean McGrath, just to name a handful. Those coaches oversaw the transition of the Mariners farm system from one of the worst in baseball to a consistent solid performer, and the pitching coaches especially helped the Mariners earn a well-deserved reputation as one of the sport's best in developing pitchers.

But the Mariners paid the price – or rather, didn't – for that innovation, losing many of their young coaches to better opportunities (and/or better salaries) elsewhere. Two that stung the most were the losses of Canham and Weiner, both of whom moved on to high-profile (and high-paid) jobs at the college level. The annual turnover started disrupting the chain of development, and the ties that bound the minor-league process to the big-league results-oriented product weakened until the cracks showed through clearly in 2026: an organization that seemed to drift without an identity or plan.

Jerry Dipoto was frank in his end-of-season presser about acknowledging the disconnect between the process the organization worked so hard to implement at the minor-league level and the big leagues, and the necessity of hiring someone who can get things back on the right track.

"We will find someone through this process who comes in and is in alignment with what we see as the direction this team needs to head in, and then guides us in a way that we get better," said Dipoto, prior to Kanzler's hiring. "Because obviously the performance on the field this year has made us question a lot of the things that we're doing at the major-league level, and I will say multiple of our major-league players who came through our system have referenced what they learned in player development that got abandoned in the big leagues this year…We need to get back to what we believe is a Mariners baseball process.

In the press conference introducing Kanzler, Justin Hollander concurred with what Dipoto had said a month earlier.

"We need to be better about connecting what we believe in organizationally to what we do in the big leagues. That's something that was identified by Jerry, myself, our baseball operations staff, and most importantly, our players as we went through the end of the season," said Hollander.

"We also need to push ourselves, try new things that maybe hadn't been on our radar. So finding someone with Jason's skillset that can develop while we're in the big leagues, that can connect an organization from top to bottom, was a huge part of this."

The Mariners of the last few years of the 2010s weren't afraid to push the envelope, to make out-of-the-box hires, and to try new things – at least as far as development-wise. Now, with hiring Kanzler, Gillman, and Swanson – ages 36, 37, and 44, respectively – they're pushing the envelope on the top rail of the dugout.

But for all the seeming risk, it's still a calculated one. Rather than make the clubhouse fit the skipper, the Mariners have chosen a skipper who fits the clubhouse.

"The uniqueness of [Kanzler's] developmental path is going to go a long way with modern players," said Jerry Dipoto.

For all the foofaraw around Kanzler's age and experience, though – or that of his bench coach or hitting coordinator – when Kanzler talks, it feels like an echo of the process the Mariners used to hire Canham a decade ago: getting the best out of players by putting the best, bigger-picture focused people in front of them.

"It's important to have connection with the players," said Kanzler in his opening presser. "It's most important to have connection throughout the entire thing. The entire organization needs to be connected towards the common goal of winning the World Series…I like to think that the sign of things going well is when people start using the word 'we' more than 'me' or 'I.' When the group starts talking in 'we', then I know that things are going well."

#2853. Greg Olsen accuses media of putting "words in my mouth" about Malik Nabers

After Sunday’s win over the Eagles, Giants receiver Malik Nabers had some things to say about things Fox’s Greg Olsen had to say about Nabers during the game.

On Monday, Olsen had some things to say about how the New York Post and USA Today characterized the things he said.

Olsen accused those publications of making up quotes and putting “worlds [sic] in my mouth.”

In order to avoid any doubt about what Olsen (and others on the Fox broadcast) said — and didn’t say — about Nabers, we went to the video (and audio).

Early in the fourth quarter of the Giants-Eagles game, after Giants quarterback J.J. McCarthy threw a pass behind an open Nabers who was running right to left, the Fox cameras showed Nabers waving an arm in apparent frustration.

Said Fox’s Joe Davis, regarding Nabers: “His body language has been terrible today.”

Olsen revisited the subject a little bit later.

“You brought up Malik Nabers,” Olsen said, “and you never know exactly what’s being said. It’s easy for us to sit here and interpret body language. When we asked [offensive coordinator] Matt Nagy about him, he said, ‘Listen, he’s very vocal. When he didn’t get the ball to start the year, he let everyone know it.’ He said, ‘Me and Malik have a good understanding right now. What’s perceived as being negative, what’s perceived as being a distraction, I think we both have an understanding of each other that allows it to coexist.’ And just certainly is something to keep an eye on.”

Not long after that, the booth threw to Fox sideline reporter Pam Oliver for more on Nabers.

“Yeah, you guys talk about his body language,” Oliver said. “I can tell you a little more about his mindset. I talked to him. And he told me, ‘I just demand excellence out of everybody around me. Anybody that has a hand in my career. Because you only get one shot. It’s your life, it’s your career.’ It’s fueled by a great sense of urgency in that hard-fought return to the field from that knee injury. Nabers said — told me, ‘I’m not going out there running all these routes not to get the ball.’”

Said Davis in response, “I totally get it. But when your teammate scores the go-ahead touchdown and you still have that look? Not great.”

“Yeah, listen,” Olsen said, “there’s a fine line. I played with a lot of really good wide receivers. A lot really good players. There’s never been a great wide receiver who hasn’t demanded the ball. That kind of comes with the territory. There is a few exceptions to guys that are just a different personality and a different approach.

“But there is a very fine line between being demanding, being a competitor, and all those great things. That’s great. It just can never cross that it feels like it takes away from the success of your teammates. So when your teammate catches a wide-open touchdown, you need to be the happiest guy on the field. You can’t still be worried about your targets. So that’s the fine line. He’s a talented, young, really good player. He’s just got to keep it in check.”

That same drive included a third-down throw to Nabers in the end zone. Nabers couldn’t make the reception, leading to a field goal.

“Listen, you want to demand the ball — you can’t throw that ball any better,” Olsen said after Nabers failed to catch the pass. “That’s a touchdown. You’re a premier player in this league. You’ve got an opportunity there, you’ve got to catch it.”

That’s the full extent of everything that was said on the Fox broadcast during the relevant exchange.

The problem is that too many outlets don’t type quotes precisely. Some will mash pieces of different sentences together. Even if the overall message is the same (and, frankly, it seems as if Davis and Olsen were being critical of Nabers, with Davis being more pointed than Olsen), the absence of accuracy opens the door for criticism of the message.

In this case, Olsen never called Nabers a “bad teammate.” However, Olsen explained that there’s a “fine line” between “being demanding, being a competitor, and all those great things” and “tak[ing] away from the success of your teammates.” Which basically is one of the hallmarks of being a bad teammate.

That’s why all outlets that are going to pass along quotes made on broadcasts should strive to get the quotes right. It eliminates any ambiguity as to what was said — and it prevents a subsequent attack regarding the accuracy of the quote.

Especially when the accurate quote tends to lead to the same place.

#2852. ’Hoos in the NBA: Early-season report card

The Virginia Cavaliers men's basketball program is enjoying an excellent start to the season. The current 'Hoos earned a statement win over No. 12 Arkansas in Washington, D.C., on Sunday afternoon to improve to 3-0, while a handful of former Virginia stars are enjoying strong starts to their seasons at the next level.

With most NBA teams reaching the 10-game checkpoint over the weekend, here's how the former faces of UVA basketball have been faring in The Association so far.

There's no better place to start than with Ty Jerome, who has become one of the NBA's feel-good stories early in the season. While Virginia fans have long known what Jerome is capable of, the national basketball audience is finally getting its formal introduction to the 6-foot-5 assassin from New Rochelle, New York.

After years of dealing with injury while intermittently posting stretches of highly efficient and majorly underappreciated play, Jerome has erupted in the early going for Memphis, averaging 26.6 points and 5.3 assists per game while piloting the Grizzlies to an unexpected 6-3 start.

The former Virginia star is playing the best basketball of his pro career, and Memphis is reaping the rewards. Jerome has already racked up two 40+ point performances this season plus a highlight reel's worth of clutch shots. Averaging a league-best 14 points per game on drives, Jerome's signature floater has become one of the league's deadliest weapons.

Coming off back-to-back seasons averaging north of 20 points per game, Trey Murphy III is back to knocking down threes and throwing down eye-popping dunks for the Pelicans.

Though his raw numbers are down slightly this season (15.6 points, 4.8 rebounds per game), his three-point efficiency (37.7%) remains sky-high.

Once again, Murphy's name is also swirling around the NBA rumor mill, frequently coming up in trade talks as New Orleans looks to rebuild. If given the chance to play for a contending team, Murphy's status as one of the league's most valuable 3-and-D weapons could finally be solidified over the back half of the 2026-27 campaign.

Now playing for his third different team in three seasons, the former fourth-overall pick has made nine starts for the Kings this season.

After an uncharacteristically poor season from three-point range in 2025-26, De'Andre Hunter is knocking down 35.3% of his attempts from downtown so far this season, propelling him to 13.1 points per game thus far. Unfortunately, despite four double-digit scoring efforts from Hunter — including back-to-back 24-point games on Oct. 27 and 29 — Sacramento is off to a frigid 1-9 start.

After a breakout campaign in Indiana last season in which he appeared in all 82 games, Jay Huff is back at it throwing down reverse slams for the Pacers.

The 7-footer, now in his sixth NBA season, has made five starts and nine total appearances this season, averaging 7.1 points per game on 49% shooting from the field. The ever-efficient Huff went off for a season-high 22 points (8-for-14 FG) and eight rebounds last Monday night against the Atlanta Hawks.

The sharp-shooting Sam Hauser continues to be one of the league's most valuable three-point specialists for the Boston Celtics. Playing 21 minutes per game (mostly off the bench, though he did start one game for Boston last week against the Milwaukee Bucks), Hauser is averaging 7.7 points per game on 40.4% shooting from the outside.

A 2024 first-round pick by Phoenix, Ryan Dunn's third professional season is off to an efficient start despite a somewhat limited role in the Suns' rotation.

The 6-foot-7 defensive ace is averaging 5.6 points, 3.6 rebounds, 1.4 stocks per game while playing 16 minutes per game for the 3-5 Suns and is coming off his best week of the season, posting a season-high 11 points in Thursday night's win against the Orlando Magic and 8 points in a weekend victory over the Houston Rockets.

With Dunn, the question is always going to be his three-point shooting. And so far this season, he's knocked down 7-of-13 attempts from distance.

The former ACC All-Defensive Team selection at Virginia in 2023-24 has yet to shoot better than 33.1% from deep in a season, but if he can keep his early-season stroke going, 'Hoos fans are well aware of the eye-popping athleticism that made Dunn such an intriguing pro prospect coming out of Charlottesville.

Beekman, now a third-year pro, signed a two-way contract with the Washington Wizards this past offseason after a year in the G-League in 2025-26. Unfortunately, Beekman is currently dealing with an acromioclavicular joint injury that will keep him sidelined for at least three weeks.

Meanwhile, the other former 'Hoo in the Nation's Capital, Anthony Gill, is the longest-tenured player on the Washington Wizards' roster. Gill has become the ultimate locker-room presence and player-coach for a franchise trying to transition from a rebuild to contention in the Eastern Conference. The seventh-year NBA vet has appeared in just two games this season, but his wisdom and steadying presence are immeasurable for the young Wizards.

#2851. Bill Belichick’s ex-girlfriend Jordon Hudson sues Mass. couple over viral Ring camera video

Hudson is suing Elise McDevitt and Craig Nolan, the owners of the property she booked through Airbnb in June 2023.

The 20-page complaint, filed on Nov. 6 in Suffolk Superior Court, states that Hudson and Belichick were keeping their relationship private at the time of the video leak.

According to the complaint, a Ring camera outside of the home captured the video of Belichick walking around shirtless.

In a text thread, Hudson wrote that McDevitt sent her the video during their stay, but assured her that it would be deleted.

The complaint states the video later appeared on social media in November 2023 and was picked up by news outlets.

Additional text threads included in the document from McDevitt show her initially denying leaking it.

McDevitt wrote, "I absolutely did not leak this video to twitter or anyone."

However, the lawsuit states that McDevitt later wrote in another text that she and Nolan shared the videos with others, writing, "We had sent it to a few people initially wondering is this him [Mr. Belichick], isn't it him."

The complaint alleged that sharing the video led to the leak and contributed to the public learning of Hudson's relationship with Belichick.

Hudson also alleged that the hosts disclosed additional information about their stay on a podcast.

The complaint also alleged the couple looked through a window during their stay.

Hudson stated she later reported the hosts to Airbnb.

Documents also alleged that McDevitt messaged Hudson, "You're not even famous now! You are infamous. Good for you!"

Hudson is seeking unspecified damages as well as a court order preventing the defendants from disclosing additional private information about her.

#2850. Bills-Vikings game carries major significance for both teams

The Bills and Vikings have identical 0-4 records in the Super Bowl. They enter Monday night’s game with identical 6-1 records.

And the stakes are very high for both teams.

For Buffalo, which hopes to force the January road to SoFi Stadium through the open-air new venue in Western New York, it’s important to maintain distance between the 6-2 Jaguars and 7-2 Broncos. (The division is looking like the Bills’ to lose, with the Patriots now at 5-4.)

For Minnesota, their status as the last unbeaten team in the NFL could dissolve to being the last team out of the NFC playoff picture.

The NFC North, which now has a 20-3 record outside the division, has the Vikings at 6-1, the Lions at 6-2, the Bears at 6-2, and the Packers at 6-3. (Combined, they’ve lost as many games as the 0-8 Chargers.)

Last week, when the Vikings picked up their first loss of the season, the Lions, Bears, and Packers won. This week, the Lions, Bears, and Packers have won. A loss by the Vikings would conclude a two-week window in which the Vikings are 0-2, and the rest of the division is 6-0.

It gets worse. The 49ers, Seahawks, and Rams each won on Sunday. In the end, five playoff berths will go to the four teams of the NFC North and top three teams of the NFC West.

Two of those teams will be left out. And, frankly, the Vikings seem destined to be one of them.

Even with a 6-2 record, if they lose to the Bills. Because even though the defense is good enough to win a Super Bowl, the offense isn’t good enough to win a playoff game. It could be just bad enough to keep the team from making the playoffs.

It was one thing for the Vikings’ defense to not gripe when the end result of each game was victory. If the second game of a four-week gauntlet doesn’t go Minnesota’s way (the Packers and 49ers are up next), things could get even more awkward in the locker room.

The problem is that, despite plenty of speculation and chatter, the Vikings don’t seem to be inclined to pull the cord on the emergency option of trading for Daniel Jones or Kirk Cousins. The organization’s chronic “just good enough is just good enough” vibe remains palpable.

Which means it all comes down to Carson Wentz against the Bills.

Could Minnesota win? When Buffalo last visited Minnesota in 2018, the Vikings entered the game as 17-point favorites. The Bills, fueled by then-rookie quarterback Josh Allen throwing for 196 yards and a touchdown and rushing for 39 yards and two touchdowns (including a hurdle over linebacker Anthony Barr), won the game easily, 27-6. (Last week, Allen said the leap over a linebacker was the moment he realized “I do belong here.”)

Four years ago, the Bills were favored by 6.5 points over the Vikings. Minnesota won a crazy game in overtime, 33-30, with Vikings receiver Justin Jefferson making the catch of the century on a fourth-down, game-on-the-line play.

Tonight, in many respects, the season is on the line for the Vikings. Especially if they won’t be trying to upgrade the most important position on the team, which in recent weeks has been the weakest on the roster.

#2847. Doc Rivers calls Sixers NBA’s best team

The mere mention of Doc Rivers' name is bound to get some (maybe most) Sixers fans feeling some kind of way. Be that as it may, Rivers was on Bill Simmons' podcast last Friday, throwing flowers in the direction of the team he coached from 2020-23.

The episode dropped before the Sixers split a pair of road games over the weekend, leaving them with a league-best 9-2 record. It is their fastest start since the Allen Iverson-led club won its first 10 games in 2000-01, en route to a Finals appearance.

"Right now," Rivers said, "they're the best team in the NBA. They're gonna be hard to beat."

The 65-year-old Rivers, whose 27-year run as a head coach appears to be at an end after he spent the last three seasons as the Milwaukee Bucks' boss, went on to say that Jaylen Brown is among the three best small forwards in the league, that Joel Embiid is no worse than the fourth-best center and that Tyrese Maxey is among the top five point guards.

"And LeBron (James), whatever he is, top five," Rivers said. "A four (i.e., power forward) right now."

"Top five defender at the 2-spot right now," Rivers said.

"I still think that, and I've said this before, anybody can go out and get the stars," he said. "It's the other guys. And I think that's where they hit the home run. … (Anfernee) Simons, (Kentavious) Caldwell-Pope, Dean Wade."

Pope, 33, is in his 14th season. He is, Rivers said, "perfect for this team, because he's winning. He's playing a role to win. Guys like Caldwell-Pope, late in their career, they can't play on a bad team. It just doesn't fit. They're trying to play right. Now he's in a perfect role."

And to the veteran coach Simons "looks better," even though his shooting has been erratic.

"He looks like this year, he's accepted his role even more," Rivers said.

Rivers reserved his greatest praise for LeBron and Maxey.

"I like LeBron's role right now," he said. "Love it."

Later, he added, "LeBron is who I would have loved him to have been early in his career (i.e., a playmaker), even though he had to score. He's the closest to Magic Johnson to ever have played, as far as being big and able to facilitate. LeBron's vision is still underrated – like, he's not a good passer; he's a great passer, and he sees it early."

James' scoring average (15.6) is far and away the lowest of his 24-year career, but he is averaging around seven assists, as well as seven rebounds.

"It's the happiest I've seen him in a long time," Rivers said. "I don't think he was ever that happy in LA (while playing for the Lakers the last eight seasons) – like this happiness. I'm not saying he wasn't happy in LA, but this is true happiness."

Maxey, meanwhile, seems content no matter the circumstances. Rivers saw that for himself when he coached him in Philadelphia, and continues to regard the young point guard as one of the most upbeat players he's ever been around.

"Maxey's one of these guys that can fit on any team," he said. "I was telling Grant Hill (the managing director of USA Basketball), if he's not on the Olympic team, you're crazy, because he's just going to do good. Even if he's not playing, he smiles. He's happy. He's competitive. And he's a phenomenal basketball player."

Rivers' wide-ranging conversation with Simmons also included a mention of former Sixer Matisse Thybulle, who has settled into a rotational role with the Lakers, his third team in eight NBA seasons.

"I'm happy for him," Rivers said. "I didn't think he loved the game enough. … He kind of was this flower child. Honestly right now, watching him play, it's like he kind of realized, 'I was almost out of the league.' … He's a smart kid, and he kind of realized, 'You know what, I've got to get cooking here.'"

Rivers called Thybulle "a hell of a defender" while adding that he "gambles too much." He also invoked the memory of the December 2021 game between the Sixers and Warriors. Entering play Stephen Curry needed 10 three-pointers to break Ray Allen's career record, but Thybulle limited him to three triples in 14 attempts, and became the first player to block two of Curry's threes in a single game. In all Curry managed 18 points on 6-for-20 shooting in the 102-93 Sixers victory.

Rivers said on the Simmons pod that while he and Curry are family – Stephen's brother Seth, another ex-Sixer, is married to Rivers' daughter Callie – he had made it clear in family discussions he did not want to see Steph break the record that night.

"It was all on Matisse," Doc said. "He guarded the hell out of him. Because Steph takes some shots, and here comes Matisse from behind, with those long arms. He recovers. So yeah, he can really defend."

#2845. Darren Waller has best game in 3 years

Week 9 was a miserable performance by the Las Vegas Raiders, falling 38-17 to the San Francisco 49ers. But let's see how the weekend fared for a few old friends: Darren Waller, Khalil Mack and Gardner Minshew.

Waller hadn't faced off against the Broncos since he was a Raider, and he made sure everyone in Denver remembered him. Not only was this the tight end's most productive outing of the season, but it was also the most receiving yards he's had in a single game since October 2023. The tight end hasn't been a huge part of Carolina's offense this season, but he is on pace to post a 500-yard, seven-touchdown campaign at 34 years old.

Mack didn't do anything in this snooze-fest of a game as the Chargers barely showed up. Instead, this is meant to be an update on the veteran's status in Los Angeles with the NFL Trade deadline coming up on Tuesday. From the sound of it, the former Raider isn't going anywhere.

"Despite their 0-7 record, the Los Angeles Chargers continue to tell teams that they have no interest in trading Khalil Mack, and the veteran pass rusher will not be going anywhere before Tuesday's deadline, according to sources," ESPN's Adam Schefter wrote on Sunday morning. "'I want Khalil Mack here,' Chargers coach Jim Harbaugh told reporters. 'Khalil Mack wants to be here.' The Chargers have prioritized keeping Mack for the leadership he provides and talent he brings, characterizing him as a pillar of the organization. They might be winless, but they are not acting that way in trade discussions regarding any of their players. Los Angeles also doesn't like the message of giving up on the season. Calls from rival general managers on Justin Herbert, Ladd McConkey and others have received hard nos."

Stats: 21/30 (70%), 166 yards, 0 TDs, 0 INTs

A week after coming off the bench and leading Arizona to a shocking comeback and upset, Minshew was named the starter and got an even bigger vote of confidence with Jacoby Brissett getting traded to the Baltimore Ravens. However, the Cardinals and Minshew didn't have as much luck this time around. To be fair, Seattle has an elite defense, and the quarterback didn't turn the ball over. However, of their nine drives, three were three-and-outs, and only two included more than six plays. Ironically, Arizona scored on its opening possession and then didn't put up any points for the rest of the game.

#2844. Lions agree to acquire S Andre Cisco from Jets

Detroit is bringing in some secondary help.

#2843. Austin Reaves keeps it real about miraculous shot: ‘B——- Luka would shoot’

DETROIT — Austin Reaves couldn't pretend it was a good shot.

Even with the Lakers star guard having the greenest of green lights after going on a personal 7-0 run as the hosts attempted to come back from down 26 points, Reaves kept it real about what followed.

Austin Reaves hit a variety of big shots in the Lakers' win over the Pistons. AP Photo/Duane Burleson

After John Collins nudged the ball out of his hands after running a pick and roll with Walker Kessler, Reaves lost possession of the ball in his left hand with his momentum taking him toward the sideline opposite of the Pistons' bench.

But he was able to get the ball back to the hottest hand on the court throughout Sunday night, regaining possession and taking a single dribble before firing up a 27-footer with 11 seconds still on the shot clock.

Austin Reaves powered the Lakers' offense with Luka Doncic sitting out against the Pistons. Getty Images

"It was a terrible shot," Reaves admitted postgame. "Absolutely."

It didn't matter, with the ball bouncing off the top of the backboard before falling through the net to give the Lakers a 78-61 lead and putting Reaves at 32 points after scoring 10 points in just over two minutes of game time for his first 30-point game of the season.

The 6-foot-5 Oklahoma product didn't even try to act like he called bank, with the shot reminding him of the kind of miraculous looks his backcourt co-star regularly attempts.

"Oh, it was 100% lucky," he said. "Honestly, when I stepped back and shot it, I was like, 'this is some b——- Luka [Dončić] would shoot.' And I didn't try to bank it in. It went in, it looks the same in the stat sheet."

Austin Reaves secured his first 40-point game of the season. NBAE via Getty Images

The 3-pointer was just one of the many wild shots Reaves made en route to scoring a season-high 43 points in the Lakers' 121-116 win over the Pistons at Little Caesars Arena.

He also hit a fadeaway from behind the backboard midway through the first quarter for his seventh and eighth points of the night which gave the Lakers an early 18-7 lead.

"Lucky shot," Reaves said. "I was a lucky player [Sunday night]."

And with Dončić, the league's leading scorer with an average of 40.1 points per game, watching from the sidelines against the Pistons because of back tightness, the Lakers greatly benefitted from Reaves' luck.

#2842. Bengals Week 9 awards in Spain victory vs. Falcons

The Cincinnati Bengals went to Spain and came home with their first-ever win outside the United States, a 34-17 handling of the Atlanta Falcons at the Bernabéu. It was their fourth straight win, it moved them to 6-2, and it put them in a tie with Pittsburgh atop the AFC North heading into next Sunday night's showdown at Paycor.

Before we get to the Steelers, let's hand out some hardware. Here are my nominees for Offensive MVP, Defensive MVP, and Unsung Hero. Then you tell me who takes home the trophy in the comments section.

Joe Burrow, QB. Burrow went 24-of-29 for 271 yards, three touchdowns, and no turnovers, good for a 140.1 passer rating. He also scored on a 1-yard sneak, giving him four total touchdowns. He was nearly flawless in the first half, going 16-of-18 for 234 yards and two scores, as the Bengals built a 24-10 halftime lead.

His 21 touchdown passes lead the NFL, and he has 16 total touchdowns over the last four games. One week after throwing three interceptions against Tennessee, he played a clean, efficient game. If the MVP race were decided today, he'd have a strong case.

Ja'Marr Chase, WR. Atlanta scored on its opening drive, and Chase answered on the Bengals' first possession by hauling in a 47-yard touchdown from Burrow to tie it at 7. He was already at eight catches for 142 yards by halftime, the most receiving yards he has ever had in a first half.

He finished with 10 catches for 155 yards on 11 targets, and his seven receiving touchdowns lead the AFC. Zac Taylor said it best after the game: "That's just Ja'Marr Chase. Best in the world."

Tee Higgins, WR. Five catches for 32 yards won't jump off the page, but Higgins was the finisher. He caught touchdowns from 4 and 8 yards out, giving him four touchdown catches in the last two games and six on the season. When the Bengals got into the red zone, Burrow looked for No. 5.

Barrett Carter, LB. Carter tied for the team lead with 10 tackles and broke up a pass. Then, with the Bengals up two scores in the fourth quarter, he picked off Michael Penix Jr. at the Atlanta 43 and effectively ended any thought of a comeback. It was his second career interception and his first of 2026.

It's been an up-and-down year for the linebackers in coverage, so seeing Carter make the game-sealing play was a welcome sight.

Bryan Cook, S. Cook also led the team with 10 tackles, five of them solo. On a day when Bijan Robinson and the Falcons' run game kept getting to the second level, Cook was the guy cleaning things up. Without him, a few of those 10-yard gains would become 30-yard gains.

Demetrius Knight Jr., LB. Knight added eight tackles, five solo, and was a steady presence in the middle of a defense that held Atlanta to seven points after the opening quarter.

Josh Newton, CB. Newton had five tackles, and the biggest play he made doesn't show up in the box score as anything more than a pass breakup. Atlanta dialed up a flea-flicker shot into the end zone, and Newton was right there to knock it away. In a game that was 24-17 in the third quarter, that's a big swing.

Dohnte Meyers, WR/KR. Meyers was all over the box score. He had two catches for 47 yards, including a 42-yarder, a 16-yard run, and four kickoff returns for 100 yards. That's 163 all-purpose yards from a guy most fans weren't talking about before Sunday. When the Bengals needed a spark outside of Chase and Higgins, Meyers gave them one.

Evan McPherson, K. McPherson drilled a 57-yarder just before halftime to make it 24-10, then added a 43-yarder in the fourth. He's now made 23 straight field goals, the longest streak in franchise history and the longest active streak in the NFL. We don't talk enough about how automatic he's been.

Samaje Perine, RB. Perine handled the dirty work with 11 carries for 43 yards. He also closed the game out the smart way: after picking up a late first down, he slid down instead of chasing extra yards, so the Bengals could run out the clock. That's veteran football.

The offensive line, led by Dalton Risner. Risner made his 100th career regular-season start, and the line paved the way for 123 rushing yards at 5.1 yards a carry. It also allowed just two sacks. After weeks of a stuck run game, the line finally won some battles up front.

Those are my nominees. Who gets your Offensive MVP: Burrow or Chase? Is Carter's game-sealing pick enough for Defensive MVP? And who's your Unsung Hero from Madrid?

The Chiefs have a clear and obvious kicking problem.

Harrison Butker officially missed one more field goal on Sunday, sailing a 51-yard attempt wide right.

But everyone also saw him miss from 56 a snap before — a play that didn’t count because of a Jets penalty.

Butker has now missed 17 kicks over the last two seasons, which is a league-high.

Kansas City has Justin Tucker on its practice squad, and he would ostensibly be the next man up. But in his Monday news conference, Reid told reporters that he wasn’t ready to commit to any changes at kicker.

#2840. Rams star Puka Nacua likely will be placed on injured reserve

The Rams' receiver corps — and the team's aspirations for another Super Bowl title — continue to sustain major hits.

Star Puka Nacua likely will be placed on injured reserve because of a right knee sprain he suffered during the Rams' victory over the Washington Commanders, coach Sean McVay said Monday.

Nacua caught 14 passes for a season-best 150 yards and a touchdown in the 20-13 victory on Sunday in Landover, Md., but he was injured after running a jet sweep on the Rams' final offensive play.

An MRI scan revealed that Nacua sprained his medial collateral and posterior cruciate ligaments. He also has a turf toe injury, McVay said.

Read more: 'I am that closer.' Myles Garrett's three-sack performance shows why Rams traded for him

The Rams' offense was attempting to close out the team's third consecutive victory with a first down that would have enabled them to run out the clock. McVay said he was "kicking myself" about calling on Nacua in that situation.

"I'd be lying to you if I said I haven't questioned the decision-making on that last play over and over in my head of what could have been another option," McVay said during a videoconference with reporters.

Nacua was sidelined two games this season because of a hip injury and was also playing through a calf injury. During the game against the Commanders, he was evaluated for a possible concussion.

If Nacua goes on injured reserve, he will join star receiver Davante Adams, who has been out since suffering a hamstring injury against the Arizona Cardinals in Week 6 at SoFi Stadium.

Players on injured reserve are required to sit out at least four games, so Nacua would miss Sunday's game against the Cardinals and games against the Green Bay Packers, Kansas City Chiefs and San Francisco 49ers before he would be eligible to return against the Dallas Cowboys on Dec. 20.

McVay said Monday he did not have a timeline for Adams' return.

Read more: Puka Nacua shines for Rams in win over Commanders, but his knee injury is a concern

"There are other guys that are very capable of doing some good stuff for us, and we'll look to see those guys step up," McVay said.

Receiver Konata Mumpfield has 11 catches, Tutu Atwell seven, Jordan Whittington two and CJ Daniels none.

McVay has said in the last few weeks that he did not foresee the Rams being active before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline. But Nacua's situation could change that.

"You've got to look at all the avenues to try to be able to solve some of the issues or situations or scenarios that have come up," he said, adding, "I would never rule it out."

Nacua was not the only player injured Sunday who could be sidelined for multiple games.

Left guard Steve Avila left against the Commanders because of a hamstring strain.

NFL teams are limited to having only eight players return from injured reserve. McVay said the Rams were "hoping to avoid" placing Avila on injured reserve but "certainly wouldn't say there's a zero chance."

Cornerback Trent McDuffie, who sat out against the Commanders because of a back injury, is making progress and "hopefully" will be available against the Cardinals, McVay said. ... After the game against the Commanders, McVay said tight end Terrance Ferguson was not activated from injured reserve as planned because of a hamstring injury suffered on Friday. "Probably be another couple weeks for him," McVay said.

#2839. Bright Side Wonders, Vol 7: The Suns have a win streak

In the third week of the NBA season, the Phoenix Suns had four games, the same number they played in the first two weeks combined. Alas, here are the questions we want your answers on for this week's edition of Bright Side Wonders:

It took them four games, but the Phoenix Suns have beaten the Houston Rockets for the first time since trading Kevin Durant to Texas. In a back-and-forth second half, the team eked out the victory thanks to an all-around scoring effort and holding the Rockets' top scorers (Durant and Alperen Şengün) under 36% shooting from the floor.

Leading as much as 19 in the first half, the Suns didn't let blowing their massive lead stop them from closing out a contested final minutes.

While the win started the team's first win streak of the season and helped build some momentum, beating Durant for the first time is noteworthy, considering the back-and-forths he's had with the franchise since his departure.

How do you feel about the Durant trade now that the Suns have finally beaten him?

After starting the year 1-5, the Phoenix Suns made a change to the starting lineup. And no, Collin Gillespie is still a reserve. Oso Ighodaro has replaced Khaman Maluach at the starting center spot, and the team is 2-0 since. The two's playing time hasn't altered much since the move, but who they're playing with has, and the Suns' starting lineup's net rating goes up by 16 points when Ighodaro is the starter, according to HoopsJunkie.

Ighodaro isn't the rebounder or the physical presence that Maluach is, but he's more experienced, and adds another playmaker to a starting lineup that lacks them. Additionally, the fact that he doesn't need to have the ball to make an impact helps a starting five full of ball-dominant scorers.

Albeit a small sample size, how are you feeling about the lineup change?

When the Suns traded for Miles Bridges, it was known that someone in the starting lineup was going to have to make a shot attempt sacrifice, but it was hard to believe it'd be Dillon Brooks after his career year and it's been him, and the Suns aren't benefiting from it.

While he's shooting career-highs from the field and three, the team is 14 points worse on offense when he's on the floor. Even though he makes up for it by being such a major impact on defense, one of the reasons the Suns surprised many last season was because of his offensive aggressiveness, and with him taking nearly five less shots per game than he did a season ago, they're struggling?

What impact would a more aggressive Brooks make on the team's offense?

The Suns' win against the Rockets was their first win against Houston in 982 days. The only other team they haven't beaten in that stretch is the Boston Celtics, who they haven't beaten since February 3rd, 2023. 1,374 days ago.

#2838. Aaron Glenn on giving up defensive play-calling: I'm looking at everything

Jets head coach Aaron Glenn took over as the team’s defensive play-caller this season and things got off to a pretty good start in September.

The Jets only allowed 30 points over the first two weeks of the season, but they’ve allowed 193 in the last seven games. That includes Sunday’s 35-point performance by the Chiefs and the Jets have only won one of those seven contests to drop to 2-7 on the season.

On Monday, Glenn was asked at a press conference if he would consider handing off the play-calling role to defensive coordinator Brian Duker in the second half of the season.

“Well, I would say this, and listen, we have not played really good defense the last couple of weeks, and I am very aware of that,” Glenn said, via a transcript from the team. “But again, there’s been evidence of us playing really good defense throughout this season and listen, I will never say never to anything, but I do know this: I’m looking at everything and I’m looking of ways of us to improve, and whatever that is for us to get better, you can best believe I’ll make those changes. And I think I’ve done that throughout my career to be able to do that is I’m not afraid to make a change. I’m not saying that is going to be the change, but I’m looking at everything.”

Given the Jets’ record, it’s hard to imagine too many little tweaks that are going to lead to a major change in circumstances for the team. It’s also hard to imagine Glenn doing much to improve his chances of sticking around after a 5-21 start to his tenure, but we’ll see if anything looks different when the Jets face the Bills at home in Week 10.

#2836. Jaguars acquire Jessie Bates III from Falcons for 2 picks, also get Davon Godchaux, Jordan Davis in trio of defensive trades

The Atlanta Falcons have apparently decided to play for the future, while the Jacksonville Jaguars are going all-in.

A day after falling to the Cincinnati Bengals 34-17 in Madrid, the Falcons reportedly traded safety Jessie Bates to the Jaguars for two draft picks, according to ESPN. Jacksonville will give up a 2027 second-round pick and a 2028 fifth-round pick in exchange for Bates.

However, that was far from the only move the Jaguars made on Monday. Shortly, the team reportedly acquired New Orleans Saints defensive tackle Davon Godchaux in a deal, according to The Ringer. The Jaguars gave up a sixth-round pick in exchange for Godchaux.

And hours after that, Jacksonville picked up another blockbuster trade, landing DT Jordan Davis while sending multiple picks to the Philadelphia Eagles.

Bates has a pretty lengthy history of success in the NFL. Though Bates has made just one Pro Bowl in his career, he's been selected to three All-Pro second teams. His most recent selection came last season, when he made 98 tackles for the Falcons.

Bates, 29, is having another excellent start in 2026. Through nine games, he's registered 60 tackles and three forced fumbles. He'll take those skills to an already-dominant Jaguars defense, which is allowing the second-fewest points per game.

It cost Jacksonville a heavy price, especially considering Bates is in the final year of his contract. Barring an extension, he'll be a free agent at the end of the 2026 season.

But Jacksonville can sense the current roster has Super Bowl upside. Thanks to a tough defense and explosive offense, the Jaguars are 6-2 coming out of Week 9. While other AFC teams have been impressive, the Jaguars might have the best all-around team in the league. The addition of Bates, along with Godchaux and Davis, should only help bolster that claim.

For the Falcons, the move signals the team will likely play for 2027. After Sunday's loss to the Bengals, the Falcons dropped to 3-6 on the season.

With the playoffs unlikely, the Falcons will start selling off any player who won't play a significant role with the team moving forward. Bates was the first — and could be the biggest — name traded as the team shifts its focus to the future.

#2834. The second half surge

Welcome to our series grading every member of the San Diego Padres' 40-man roster. The roster being evaluated is current as of Oct. 18, prior to the end of the World Series. Because of that, it will include players who will be free agents once the postseason concludes. Each player will be graded on their performance in the 2026 season, including the playoffs. That performance will be weighted based on how much time the player spent with the team (availability is the best ability).

When Jackson Merrill trotted out to center field on Opening Day of the 2024 season, he had played just six games at the position. Merrill was drafted by San Diego as a lifetime shortstop, but transitioned to center field and flourished. In his rookie year, Merrill finished runner-up in NL Rookie of the Year voting behind Pittsburgh Pirates ace Paul Skenes. He had a bit of a slump in his sophomore season, being held back by injury.

However, 2026 was a return to form. Merrill recorded the first 20/20 season of his young career, finishing the year with a career-high 26 homers and 27 stolen bases. If not for a slightly unproductive first half of the season, Merrill might have recorded the first 30/30 season in Padres' franchise history. Along with Fernando Tatis Jr., Merrill buoyed the Padres' second-half surge.

Merrill's overall metrics have consistently declined since his rookie year. The center fielder's batting average, on-base percentage, slugging percentage and OPS have all decreased each year. Still, 2026 was his most productive season yet with a career-high in combination of runs scored and runs batted in (173).

There's a lot to suggest that Merrill was unlucky. His .270 expected batting average was 26 points higher than his actual mark (.254) while his .491 xSLG was 51 points above his actual (.440). Much of that may have to do with Merrill's unbelievably high strikeout rates (career-high 160 strikeouts). If he can mitigate the Ks in 2027, Merrill could be phenomenal leader in the San Diego clubhouse this coming season.

#2833. Basketball puberty? The Raptors’ growing pains and breakouts through the road trip

Teenage life is complicated, and while we're in the 2020s, it seems that the Toronto Raptors are going through an awkward phase right now. With a big brother (Kawhi) out of the house for college (injury treatment), they're having to find themselves in a big and scary high school (Eastern Conference) as they're trying to develop an identity for themselves in an unforgiving world (the National Basketball Association).

More threes, and a Kawhi Leonard and Scottie Barnes centred offence, would have both been logical progressions from last year's Raptors' squad. But, Leonard out and some streaky shooting has meant that the Raptors are looking like quite a different beast entirely. Tonight, the Raptors are matching up with the Grizzlies, a familiar foe from the Vancouver days, with the possibility to have three starters out, due to Leonard's injury management and some issues exhibited from Scottie Barnes and Immanuel Quickley.

Barnes and Quickley are both focal parts of the Raptors' offence, but didn't play in the team's last game, a victory against the Milwaukee Bucks. Across the four game road trip, the Raps went two and two, picking up wins against two really, really bad teams (Clippers and Bucks) and losing to a great team and a fine team (Lakers and Nuggets). Not super heartening, but again, the first major road trip, and all of those games were played with at least one starter down.

The road trip continued to showcase Scottie Barnes' development into a type of player that we only saw glimpses of during the last playoffs. Barnes, this season, has transformed himself into a rebounding, midrange shooting, volume scorer as opposed to the pass-first point forward that he had developed a reputation as.

As of time of writing, Scottie is second in the league for midranges attempted, and third in the league for midranges made. This is, quite frankly, bananas, considering that Barnes was never known to be a primary scorer; it seems that one summer with Kawhi Leonard has rubbed off a lot on the younger player. Barnes has never been more fun to watch, while still maintaining his responsibilities on the defensive end, averaging 2.3 steals on the season so far.

Barnes has shown that he's able to do something that superstars are often called upon to do: prop up a mediocre team with great scoring, taking over like he did during the win against the Clippers. And indeed, the Raptors have thus far been unfortunately mediocre.

"We've been very underwhelming to start the season," said Jamal Shead after the Raptors' win against the Bucks last week. "We have so much more to put out, and I think today was a start," he continued.

Consistency is a big part of that. Kristian Cuaresma pointed to the loss to the Denver Nuggets being a tale of four quarters: the Raptors take 12 threes in the first quarter, and make six. Immanuel Quickley gets 10 points and six times in 12 minutes. But then, the Raptors shoot 6-22 from three for the rest of the game, and Immanuel Quickley ends the game, scoring just three more points, and getting one more assist.

In the game against the Los Angeles Clippers, Barnes finished with 22, but only had seven before the fourth quarter.

And, in the game against the Bucks, Jakob Poeltl plays 31 points and gets a 19 point, 11 rebound double-double. Yes, the Bucks are abysmal, but when Poeltl does that in one game, but has a rough stretch like he does at the beginning of the season, it's difficult to know what version of the big man the Raptors are getting on a night-to-night basis, at this juncture.

Understanding team identity is important, and clearly, the idea of Scottie Barnes as a point forward supporting Kawhi Leonard, and the Raptors developing a major three point shooting offence isn't developing quite as anyone thought it might. And ultimately, that's okay. But this lack of dependable production (and availability) from just about everyone on the Raptors means that every game is shaping up to be a very different kind of contest, where weapons end up being used in majorly different ways.

If Darko Rajaković understands, and is able to harness his players' varying levels of production going forward, there won't be an issue. But, the alternative, a confusing and inconsistent team, would be devastating after making such a big swing for Kawhi Leonard.

Jamal Shead might have been speaking for his team with his statement: the Raptors can be doing a lot more, and perhaps will be able to turn the corner now into a more polished version of what they bring to the hardwood. But finally cementing the identity of this iteration of the Raptors is a project that seems far from finished. Growing up takes a lot of time, and a lot of pain for everyone involved, and Toronto seems to be no exception.

#2830. Todd Monken not ready to name starter at QB for Week 10

Shedeur Sanders has given Todd Monken something to think about.

The Browns’ head coach told reporters in his Monday news conference that he’s not ready to name a starter at quarterback for the Week 10 matchup against the Texans.

That came after Jeremy Fowler of ESPN reported that Deshaun Watson would be back in the saddle after missing Sunday’s game.

“I really haven’t thought through it other than getting through this morning’s tape,” Monken said. “I just got done meeting with the guys. They’ll flush the game and then we’ll have a chance to meet offensively more [extensively] later today. We went through the tape, immediately had to break, go to personnel, then the team comes in, and we haven’t had a chance to circle back as an offensive staff.”

Watson started the first eight games of the season for Cleveland, generating five game-winning drives for the team’s five wins.

Sanders, who started after Watson missed the first two days of the practice week while in concussion protocol, completed 19-of-31 passes for 297 yards with two touchdowns and no picks against the Saints.

“Well I’m awfully proud of him,” Monken said of Sanders. “It’s been 10 weeks since he had reps with the ones. I think it showed up, the reps that we gave him in camp.

“I thought he battled. I thought he showed composure in the pocket, getting to his check-downs at times. He made plays with his feet in escaping. He made the scramble play to Jerry [Jeudy]. He made a big third-down conversion early and he made one right there in four-minute, right before the holding call that gave us a first down. So, that’s what you wanted to see.”

Monken noted that philosophically, he doesn’t really think of a player losing a job due to injury.

“It’s week to week,” Monken said. “A player doesn’t lose or win a job — I don’t know what that means. Each week, we put the best players on the field that give us a chance to win.”

Monken added that Sanders’ play didn’t necessarily alter his thinking about whether or not Sanders could play at some point this season.

“I was thrilled. I was happy for him,” Monken said. “Every player wants to go and play and play well. And there were moments [where] I didn’t coach well enough, he didn’t play well enough. But there were moments at the end of the game where he made plays, right? He got it in the players’ hands, did a really good job, was in command. So, I was really pleased for him. And I was hopeful he’d play well. I thought he had a great week of practice and there is no guarantee that will carry over. So I’m really happy for him.

“Does it give me confidence? From what I saw in the fall, you bet it does. It gives me more and more confidence that what I saw in the fall, there’s something to build on — for sure.”

We’ll see how that translates as the season continues. But the fact that Sanders played well enough to make it an open question does say something about his development.

#2827. Another trade! Jaguars land their defensive game-changer in Jordan Davis

If there was any lingering doubt about the Jacksonville Jaguars' ambitions this season, James Gladstone has just put it to bed.

The Jaguars have agreed a blockbuster trade with the Philadelphia Eagles for defensive tackle Jordan Davis, sending a 2027 first-round pick and a 2029 second-round pick to Philadelphia in exchange for the former first-round selection.

It's the third defensive addition Jacksonville has made in a remarkable day of activity, following the acquisitions of Davon Godchaux from New Orleans and Jessie Bates III from Atlanta.

But this is a different level of investment. Jacksonville hasn't simply dipped into its draft capital to patch up a few holes. It has surrendered two premium selections to acquire a 26-year-old defensive lineman who could become a cornerstone of its defense for years to come.

The Jaguars aren't just buying to secure a place in the playoffs. They're buying for a Super Bowl.

There aren't many players in the NFL who can physically dominate the interior of an offensive line quite like Jordan Davis.

Standing at 6-foot-6 and weighing 336 pounds, Davis is an imposing presence who brings an entirely different dimension to Jacksonville's defensive front.

Selected 13th overall in the 2022 NFL Draft, Davis arrived in Philadelphia with enormous expectations. His development has seen him become an important component of one of the league's most formidable defensive fronts, earning a Super Bowl ring along the way.

His 2025 campaign was arguably his best yet. Davis recorded career highs of 72 tackles, 4.5 sacks and nine tackles for loss, demonstrating that he could offer more than simply occupying blockers and clogging running lanes.

He's registered 31 tackles, 1.5 sacks and two tackles for loss through nine starts this season. Those numbers don't necessarily jump off the page, but Davis' value extends beyond the traditional box score. His ability to occupy multiple blockers, control the line of scrimmage and create opportunities for the players around him makes him an incredibly valuable defensive asset.

For defensive coordinator Anthony Campanile, adding a player of Davis' physical profile could be transformational. The Jaguars have already lost Davon Hamilton for the season, creating an obvious need in the middle of their defensive line. Godchaux provides an experienced replacement, but Davis offers something considerably more exciting: a potential long-term answer at the position.

And there's a further attraction. Davis has spent his entire professional career playing in Philadelphia's defensive system under Vic Fangio, giving Jacksonville another player with experience in a defensive environment that shares some philosophical similarities with Campanile's approach.

Let's not pretend this is a cheap acquisition.

A first-round pick in 2027 and a second-round selection in 2029 is an enormous amount of draft capital to surrender for one defensive player. Especially when you consider that Chicago recently paid two first-round picks to acquire Tennessee Titans defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons.

But context matters. Jacksonville is 6-2, sitting atop the AFC South and playing some outstanding football. Its offense is among the league's best, while Campanile has developed a defense capable of competing with anybody.

The opportunity is there. The Jaguars have recognised it, and they're acting accordingly.

There's also a longer-term consideration. Davis is only 26 and was signed to a three-year, $78 million contract extension by Philadelphia in March. This isn't a rental player brought in for a desperate postseason push. Jacksonville is acquiring a player who can help them now and remain an important part of the defensive front beyond this season.

The first-round pick could ultimately be a relatively late selection if the Jaguars continue on their current trajectory. That doesn't make it insignificant, but it does change the calculation. At some point, teams have to stop treating draft picks as lottery tickets and start using them to improve a roster capable of winning a championship.

This is a question that needs answering. Whenever a blue-chip player gets moved from one roster to another, there has to be a reason, right?

The Eagles have invested heavily in Davis, handing him a three-year, $78 million extension earlier this year. He's also been an important leader in their locker room, making his departure all the more surprising.

However, Philadelphia's season has taken a significant turn for the worse. The Eagles have been struggling, and moving a player of Davis' value for two premium draft selections gives Howie Roseman an opportunity to reshape the roster.

The return is substantial. Philadelphia now has an additional first-round pick in 2027 and another second-round selection in 2029, giving Roseman even more ammunition to replenish a roster that has some significant questions to answer.

There is also the situation surrounding Moro Ojomo. Philadelphia had reportedly been reluctant to move him, and Davis' departure potentially creates a clearer path towards retaining the younger defensive tackle.

It's a significant financial and football decision, particularly after extending Davis just eight months ago. But Roseman has built his reputation on knowing when to sell, and he's extracted a substantial return here.

The Eagles may ultimately be comfortable with the decision. Jacksonville certainly will be hoping they regret it.

There's always a danger in handing out top grades for blockbuster trades. Draft capital matters, and surrendering a first and second-round pick is a considerable commitment. But the Jaguars have a genuine opportunity to do something special this season.

They've lost important defensive starters to injury, identified the positions that need strengthening and gone out to acquire established NFL talent. Godchaux brings experience. Bates brings leadership and playmaking ability. Davis brings size, power, youth and the potential to anchor the defensive interior for the foreseeable future.

Most importantly, these additions complement a roster that was already performing at an exceptionally high level. James Gladstone and Liam Coen clearly believe this team is ready to compete for the biggest prize in football. Rather than sitting back and hoping their existing roster is good enough, they've invested heavily in making sure it is.

This is what going ALL IN looks like. The Jaguars have made their intentions abundantly clear, and the rest of the league have just been put on notice.

#2823. Kon Knueppel's latest injury setback could be crushing blow for Hornets

The start to this NBA season just went from bad to worse for the Charlotte Hornets.

Kon Knueppel is expected to miss an extended amount of action after reaggravating the same left hamstring injury that forced him to miss the beginning of the 2026-27 NBA season. The latest setback was particularly cruel, just six minutes into his season debut for the Hornets.

Charlotte had been eagerly awaiting Knueppel's return to the lineup after he led the NBA in 3-pointers and finished runner-up to former Duke teammate Cooper Flagg in Rookie of the Year voting. But the latest update from team officials confirmed Knueppel won't be back on the court soon to help a Hornets team in a rough patch earlier than expected this season.

Here's the latest on Knueppel's injury situation and how it affects the Hornets:

NBA ROOKIE RANKINGS: Top player so far wasn't a top-10 pick

The Hornets announced Sunday that Knueppel has been diagnosed with a left hamstring strain and will be reevaluated in four weeks. The injury is a recurrence of the issue that sidelined him for the first eight games of the season.

The team had initially termed Knueppel's injury as "left leg soreness" when he left the Hornets' Nov. 4 game against the Brooklyn Nets.

Although there didn't appear to be a specific play in which Knueppel got hurt, he exited shortly after he dribbled the ball off his foot. During the play, Knueppel tried to dribble the ball behind his back, but it caromed off his right leg, forcing him to take a small step as he tried to recover it. Immediately after the play, Knueppel limped but tried to stay in the game before quickly heading to the bench. He eventually went into the locker room and did not return.

Knueppel scored 3 points, all from free throws, missed his only shot attempt, a 3-pointer, and committed 2 turnovers in 5:51 on the floor against the Nets.

Knueppel's return seemed to be coming at the perfect time for the Hornets, who had been reeling from a barrage of injuries early on this season. Instead, Knueppel is out for the foreseeable future and the Hornets have lost much of the momentum they generated last season.

Charlotte (3-7) enters Monday's game against the Cleveland Cavaliers on a six-game losing streak. It has already been without key players like Brandon Miller (strained right hip), Grant Williams (right hamstring strain), Dennis Schroder (left hamstring strain), center Moussa Diabate (right quad tendinopathy) and guard Coby White (left calf) due to injury through 10 games this season.

The lack of continuity is in stark contrast to last season, when the Hornets' top eight scorers all played at least 65 games and they were one of the NBA's biggest surprises. Charlotte finished with the franchise's best record in a decade (44-38) and qualified for the Eastern Conference play-in tournament as the No. 9 seed. Knueppel played 81 games as a rookie.

The injuries have also exposed the risks that came with the Hornets' offseason decision to trade LaMelo Ball. Knueppel's emergence as a rookie helped justify the move, but Charlotte's roster looks far different when he's unavailable. It certainly doesn't work without Miller, White and Diabate, too.

The good news is the bottom of the Eastern Conference appears soft. The Hornets, as much as they've struggled so far, still sit in 10th place in the standings. They might be able to stay in the postseason race without Knueppel for a month, particularly if Miller and Williams are back.

But if his absence lasts beyond Christmas, the Hornets could be staring at a lost season after finally seeming to find themselves over the past year.

#2821. Kevin Stefanski on Jessie Bates trade: Falcons aren't punting on the season

The Falcons traded veteran safety Jessie Bates to the Jaguars today, which some might see as a sign that the Falcons don’t see themselves as contenders and are looking ahead at 2027. Falcons head coach Kevin Stefanski says that’s not the case.

Stefanski did acknowledge that the Falcons are building for the future, but he also noted that in the NFC South, Atlanta remains very much in contention at 3-6.

“You guys know how I feel about Jessie the person, Jessie the player,” Stefanski told reporters. “But at the same time, this is football, where sometimes you have one foot in the short term, one foot in the long term. There’s things like this that make your team better in the long run. Draft picks are the life blood of an organization. That’s something we believe in.

Stefanski said he wants fans to know that they still are going to compete to win the NFC South.

“Our fans are smart enough to know that in no way, shape or form would we punt on this season. We have three wins, just like the rest of our division. Obviously not where we want to be, we want to play better, but we’re in the thick of this thing and we trust in the guys we have,” Stefanski said.

The Falcons might not be punting on the season, but they are deciding to move on from a player who can help them this season and get draft picks for future seasons. The Falcons still might win the worst division in football, but today’s trade is not the trade of a team that thinks it’s a Super Bowl contender this year.

#2820. Dodgers Re-Sign Versatile Infielder Who Could Earn 2027 Roster Spot

The Los Angeles Dodgers inked Tyler Fitzgerald to a minor league deal, completing their first official free-agent signing, though it keeps an internal depth piece on the team.

Fitzgerald came over from the Toronto Blue Jays in a late-April trade that sent cash to the AL East team for the utility player.

He played in 24 games for the Oklahoma City Comets in Triple-A last year, slotting in at third base, left field, second base, shortstop, and right field.

The former Blue Jay hit .293, hit six home runs, drove in 22 RBIs, and posted an OPS of .998.

Aug 17, 2025; San Francisco, California, USA; San Francisco Giants second baseman Tyler Fitzgerald (49) runs the bases after hitting a one run home run against the Tampa Bay Rays during the seventh inning at Oracle Park. Mandatory Credit: Robert Edwards-Imagn Images

The Dodgers designated Fitzgerald for assignment in the middle of 2026, though it was more of a procedural move, since they quickly re-signed him to a minor league deal later, simply adding a spot on the 40-man roster.

Miguel Rojas's retirement naturally opens up a slot in the infield, and rather than add an outright infielder, Fitzgerald is a possible utility-player option.

Additionally, Kike Hernandez is also a utility player, but he is a free agent. The only outright positionally flexible players are Tommy Edman and Hyeseong Kim.

Feb 13, 2025; Glendale, AZ, USA; Los Angeles Dodgers infielder Kike Hernandez looks on during a Spring Training workout at Camelback Ranch Mandatory Credit: Joe Camporeale-Imagn Images Credit: USA TODAY Sports via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

If Hernandez departs and the Dodgers don't add a meaningful utility player via free agency, Fitzgerald will certainly get a run at taking a spot on the active roster, or at least remain on the fringes.

Fitzgerald is only two years removed from a .280/.334/.497 season with the San Francisco Giants, where he put up 2.7 bWAR in 96 games.

There is a chance the Dodgers see him tapping back into that kind of production, which came with really good defense, and becoming another utility-player steal.

#2816. JJ Redick was impressed with the Lakers’ poise in win over Pistons

Despite Luka Dončić's absence, the Los Angeles Lakers won their fifth straight game Sunday night with a 121-116 victory over the Detroit Pistons and moved to 9-2 on the young season. Spearheaded by a 43-point performance from Austin Reaves, their offense led the way as they recorded a 127.4 offensive rating — their fourth-highest mark of the year, per Cleaning the Glass — against Detroit's third-ranked defense.

The Lakers roared out to 38-14 advantage after the first quarter before scoring just 50 points the next two periods. Then, as the Pistons inched closer, they slammed the door shut with a 33-point final frame to secure the win.

"Love the way we started the game. There was a tremendous amount of, even in the pregame tape, a tremendous amount of focus on our part on handling their activity, pressure, their fouling, all of that stuff," head coach JJ Redick told reporters following the win. "Outside of the third, and particularly, those four straight possessions in the third, I thought we did a good job of that. Overall, having three turnovers in that fourth quarter, when they were really trying to ramp up their defense, we just showed a lot of poise throughout."

Among the hallmarks of the Pistons' stingy defense is their ability to generate takeaways — they rank third in opposing turnover rate at 17.9% this season and were first a year ago. Even without Dončić, the Lakers managed to dip below Detroit's season average and produced a 16.8% turnover rate.

They've had plenty better showings this year (five, to be exact) but it's a vastly improved number compared to their other Dončić-less outing, when they turned the ball over 23.3% of the time against the Los Angeles Clippers on Oct. 28. For his part, Reaves was superbly economical and committed just one turnover on his 79 touches Sunday.

The West-leading Lakers will aim for a sixth consecutive win Monday night against the 7-4 Atlanta Hawks, which are riding their own four-game winning streak and tout the NBA's fourth-highest opposing turnover rate. Los Angeles will hope this one features Dončić back in the fold. Tipoff is scheduled for 4 p.m. PST.
